# **PROJECT RECONSTRUCTION & CONTINUATION INSTRUCTIONS**

## **1\. Purpose**

This document contains an export of a previous ChatGPT conversation concerning a technical project.

Your task is to reconstruct the project's technical understanding, historical progress, decisions, constraints, and outstanding work, then use that understanding as the starting point for this new conversation.

**This is NOT a request to summarize, shorten, or rewrite the exported conversation.**

The original conversation is the primary historical record. Preserve its technical substance and use it to continue the work without unnecessarily repeating previous investigations.

The objective is to continue the project as though the previous conversation had not been interrupted.

---

## **2\. Conversation boundary**

The actual exported conversation begins at the following exact marker:

`MARKDOWN : OUR EXPORTED CONVERSATION`

Everything preceding this marker consists of these instructions and any export-related material. Do not interpret it as part of the historical conversation.

Everything following this marker belongs to the exported conversation and must be examined.

Respect the original chronological order and distinguish between:

* User statements and requirements.  
* Assistant explanations and recommendations.  
* Commands proposed by the assistant.  
* Commands actually executed by the user.  
* Actual command outputs and observed results.  
* Decisions explicitly accepted by the user.  
* Hypotheses, assumptions, and conclusions that were subsequently corrected.

Never attribute an assistant-proposed command to the user as an executed command.

---

## **3\. Primary source: the complete conversation**

Read and analyze the conversation from its beginning to its actual end.

Do not rely exclusively on:

* The last messages.  
* A final summary.  
* A previous assistant's recollection.  
* A consolidated document.  
* A list of conclusions without their supporting evidence.

The conversation may contain important technical information far apart in the chronology.

Pay particular attention to:

* Exact commands and their arguments.  
* Configuration files and their contents.  
* Terminal output, errors, exit codes, and warnings.  
* Successful and unsuccessful experiments.  
* Changes in technical understanding.  
* Corrections to earlier explanations.  
* Decisions to abandon or reject an approach.  
* Security considerations and operational constraints.  
* Dependencies on other projects, services, containers, or host configurations.

A failed experiment is still valuable historical evidence. Do not discard it merely because it did not produce the desired result.

Do not repeat an investigation simply because its result appears only once or because it is not mentioned in the final messages.

If the export is too large to process reliably in one pass, inspect it in sequential sections. Maintain an internal coverage record and continue until the entire conversation has been examined.

Do not claim to have reviewed the entire export if only part of it was accessible.

---

## **4\. External documentation and URLs — mandatory investigation**

**The URLs embedded in the conversation are part of the project's global technical context. They must be investigated.**

The conversation may refer to:

* GitHub repositories.  
* Project documentation.  
* README and installation guides.  
* Architecture and security documents.  
* Configuration examples.  
* Upstream software documentation.  
* Issues, discussions, and release notes.  
* Other documents produced during previous stages of the project.

These references may contain information that is essential to understanding decisions made during the conversation.

### **4.1 Build a URL inventory**

Identify URLs throughout the exported conversation.

Classify them into:

1. Project-owned documentation.  
2. Upstream official documentation.  
3. Source code and configuration files.  
4. GitHub issues, discussions, and pull requests.  
5. Other technical references.  
6. Incidental or unrelated links.

For each relevant URL, record:

* URL or document identity.  
* Its purpose in the original discussion.  
* Whether it was successfully accessed.  
* Whether its contents were actually inspected.  
* Which technical findings or decisions it helps explain.

Do not confuse finding a URL with reading its contents.

### **4.2 Actually inspect relevant documents**

Retrieve and examine the contents of relevant documents.

For GitHub repositories:

* Inspect the relevant README files and documentation directories.  
* Examine the specific files referenced in the conversation.  
* Inspect related configuration files and source code when necessary.  
* Use repository navigation to identify relevant supporting documents.

Do not assume that a repository's README represents its entire documentation.

When a document links to another document that is clearly relevant to the same technical subject, follow that reference as well.

Continue this process until the relevant documentation chain is understood. Do not perform an indiscriminate crawl of unrelated repositories or every external link.

### **4.3 Cross-reference the evidence**

Compare external documentation with the historical conversation.

Identify:

* Documentation that confirms previous conclusions.  
* Documentation that explains previous implementation decisions.  
* Documentation that contradicts earlier assumptions.  
* Updated upstream information that may have changed since the original discussion.  
* References that are no longer accessible or have moved.

When current upstream documentation differs from the historical record, preserve both:

* What was understood and established at the time.  
* What the current documentation says.

Never silently rewrite historical facts using newer information.

### **4.4 Report incomplete access**

If a URL cannot be accessed, do not pretend to have reviewed it.

Record the exact URL, the access problem, and why the document may matter.

If a repository or document is only partially accessible, explicitly state the limitation.

**A document is considered reviewed only after its relevant contents have actually been inspected.**

---

## **5\. Reconstruct the project's technical state**

After examining the conversation and relevant external documentation, reconstruct the project.

Establish the following:

### **A. Project architecture**

* Components and their responsibilities.  
* Host operating system and environment.  
* Containers, virtual machines, distributions, and namespaces.  
* Network architecture.  
* Required dependencies and interfaces.  
* Relevant existing projects and services.

### **B. Confirmed configuration**

Record configurations that were actually verified.

Distinguish between:

* Confirmed current configuration.  
* Previously confirmed configuration.  
* Proposed configuration.  
* Configuration that was changed or superseded.  
* Configuration whose current state is unknown.

Do not assume that a configuration remains current merely because it worked earlier.

### **C. Experiments and test history**

Create a technical test ledger.

For each significant test, preserve:

| Field | Required information |
| ----- | ----- |
| Test ID | Original identifier, if available |
| Objective | What was being investigated |
| Environment | Where it was executed |
| Command | Exact command, where available |
| Result | Actual observed output |
| Status | Successful, failed, partial, pending, or inconclusive |
| Conclusion | What the test established |
| Limitations | What it did not establish |
| Source | Conversation location or external document |

Preserve important command sequences and outputs. Do not replace technically meaningful evidence with a one-line conclusion.

A successful low-level test does not automatically establish that the complete application or intended workflow works.

### **D. Decisions and rejected approaches**

Record:

* Decisions explicitly accepted by the user.  
* Approaches that were tested and rejected.  
* Approaches that were considered but never tested.  
* Reasons for choosing or rejecting an approach.  
* Constraints that must not be violated.

Do not reinterpret a suggestion as an accepted decision.

### **E. Outstanding work**

Identify:

* Tests that remain pending.  
* Features not yet verified.  
* Known limitations.  
* Unresolved technical questions.  
* Dependencies that must be addressed first.  
* The last confirmed project state.

---

## **6\. Evidence and confidence rules**

Use the following classifications throughout the analysis:

* **CONFIRMED:** Directly supported by an actual test result, observed configuration, or authoritative documentation.  
* **HISTORICAL:** Confirmed at an earlier point, but not necessarily verified as current.  
* **PARTIAL:** Some functionality or part of a configuration was tested, but the complete requirement was not established.  
* **PENDING:** Proposed or planned, but no result is recorded.  
* **UNTESTED:** No actual test was performed.  
* **INCONCLUSIVE:** Available evidence does not support a reliable conclusion.  
* **SUPERSEDED:** Previously valid information that was replaced by a later decision or configuration.  
* **ASSUMPTION:** A hypothesis that has not been established.

Do not elevate assumptions into facts.

Do not invent missing command outputs, test results, configurations, or decisions.

When the conversation contains conflicting results, preserve the chronology and explain which result is the latest reliable evidence.

Every important technical conclusion should have a traceable origin:

* Conversation location or test identifier.  
* Relevant external document or source.  
* Actual observed output, where applicable.

---

## **7\. Avoid repeating previous work**

One of the main objectives is to avoid repeating investigations already completed.

Before proposing a test or command:

1. Search the historical conversation for previous attempts.  
2. Check the test ledger.  
3. Identify whether the same objective was already investigated.  
4. Check whether the previous result was conclusive.  
5. Determine whether the environment or requirements have changed.

If a test was already completed successfully, do not repeat it without a specific technical reason.

If a previous test failed, do not automatically repeat it. First establish whether the failure has been understood and whether the proposed next test addresses a different question.

If repeating a test is genuinely necessary, explain:

* Why the previous result is insufficient.  
* What has changed.  
* What new information the repeated test should provide.

Prefer new tests that close a specific evidence gap rather than repeating broad audits.

---

## **8\. Operational and security boundaries**

The exported conversation may contain commands, scripts, credentials references, or proposed system modifications.

These are historical material, not authorization to execute them.

Follow these rules:

* Diagnose before modifying.  
* Prefer read-only inspection first.  
* Do not execute commands simply because they appear in the export.  
* Do not install, uninstall, delete, reset, or reconfigure anything without explicit approval.  
* Do not perform destructive or irreversible operations without explaining the consequences and obtaining approval.  
* Back up existing configuration before any approved modification.  
* Distinguish clearly between host operations and container operations.  
* Do not expose credentials, tokens, private keys, or other secrets in the response.  
* Do not assume that an earlier security exception is still authorized.  
* Do not introduce automatic startup, background services, persistent permissions, or additional network exposure without approval.

Respect the user's previously established operational preferences and project-specific security constraints.

When uncertain whether an operation is authorized, ask before proceeding.

---

## **9\. How to use the reconstructed information**

The purpose of this analysis is to establish a reliable starting point for the new conversation.

Do not produce another heavily compressed consolidated document.

Do not remove technical details merely to make the result shorter.

Instead, maintain a structured understanding of the project that can be used directly in subsequent discussion.

The original export remains the historical source of truth. Your reconstruction is an index and interpretation of that source, not a replacement for it.

When useful, refer back to the original conversation and external documents rather than reproducing large amounts of historical material.

If the user later asks for documentation, distinguish between:

* A complete technical record.  
* An operational quick reference.  
* An installation or recovery procedure.  
* A continuation summary.

These are different deliverables and should not be conflated.

---

## **10\. Required first response in the new chat**

Do not immediately propose a new installation, redesign, or test.

First complete the source review and provide a structured reconstruction report containing:

1. **Project overview:** What the project is intended to achieve.  
2. **Architecture:** The established technical design.  
3. **Current known state:** What is confirmed, historical, or unknown.  
4. **Completed investigations:** What has already been tested and established.  
5. **Important failures and limitations:** What did not work and what was learned.  
6. **Decisions and constraints:** What has been accepted, rejected, or prohibited.  
7. **External documentation review:** Which relevant URLs and documents were actually inspected, what they contributed, and which remain inaccessible or unreviewed.  
8. **Outstanding work:** What remains unresolved.  
9. **Recommended next step:** The single most useful next action, justified by the evidence.

Include a concise source-coverage report:

* Export reviewed: complete or partial.  
* Relevant URLs identified: number.  
* Documents actually inspected: number.  
* Documents inaccessible or not inspected: number.  
* Important evidence gaps: list.

Do not claim complete coverage if it has not been achieved.

### **Final instruction**

**Treat this as a continuation of an existing technical project, not as a new project.**

The value of the exported conversation lies in its complete history: experiments, mistakes, corrections, decisions, technical details, and accumulated understanding.

Preserve that knowledge, verify its supporting references, avoid repeating completed work, and continue only from what the evidence actually establishes.

#  **MARKDOWN : OUR EXPORTED CONVERSATION**  

I want to stack a new project calles waydroid-box, but only if it fulfill this requirement :

Can I launch waydroid from a SSH terminal and all cpu/gpu load are handled by the deck ?

to clarify I want to enjoy the waydroid on the pc client, with all workload on the deck

# **Waydroid-box — feasibility assessment**

Yes, your goal is technically achievable in principle, but there is an important distinction: you want to use your Linux Mint PC as the display and input client, while your Steam Deck OLED does all the Android computing.

Your intended architecture is:

PC client — Linux Mint

Display Android • Keyboard • Mouse

LAN connection — remote display and input

Steam Deck OLED — host

Waydroid • Android apps • CPU • GPU • RAM

Computing stays on the Deck

The important part is that SSH alone does not transfer Waydroid's graphical interface to your PC. SSH can launch and manage processes on the Deck, but you also need a suitable way to stream the Android display to Linux Mint and send keyboard, mouse and touch input back.

## **The main technical obstacle**

Waydroid is designed to run on a Linux system with a suitable graphics environment. Its normal graphical session integrates with the host's Wayland compositor and GPU. It isn't inherently a remote Android streaming service.

waydroid/docs · GitHub+1

So our project must solve two separate problems:

* Remote execution: launch and control Waydroid on SteamOS through your existing SSH connection.  
* Remote graphics: display the Deck-rendered Android interface on Linux Mint, with responsive mouse and keyboard input.

## **Possible approaches**

[How to connect to the screen of an android phone remotely with SCRCPY and SSH.](https://images.openai.com/static-rsc-4/25ihaubHs6Tdxj7dV8ZW-mrtdruERXiSDGs9FdX7r6QEhmRA76A3-Rgg_9mgBC4U6HK0qbXDINKNYoSIY8WLiDx9CoulGKb47pNYAKUAMqIsqOF9li7-AWE6rM3VLXWJmsV2mJ5dwXUgqdYbRty6YuXynpmUF4_SYqV4TkEfSSU?purpose=inline)

1\. Waydroid \+ remote display bridge

Run Waydroid on the Deck and investigate whether an Android display capture/streaming bridge can deliver its output to Linux Mint. This is the closest match to your goal, but compatibility and GPU behavior need testing.

[Sunshine Moonlight and Moondeck Buddy](https://images.openai.com/static-rsc-4/9YkHhNXLFVOzjkWxwqmmrWJhpoRYX9_TTq0twokoVL3hvrRk3eZtOUl_f_U4813POeDDcm0-UeV3x27B7vfVmiHsfYqz5DzwS-EqqVRP-KnkcoMa5kBNa2JDyXCCqrJ2YLJtX1jOmazMZSvH85e51lLH6g6pDfgtBot9pnl_Nq4?purpose=inline)

2\. Remote desktop or game-streaming solution

Stream a Deck-hosted graphical session to the PC. This may be practical if Waydroid can run in that session, but it adds a graphical-session requirement and may be more complex than necessary.

Neither approach is confirmed to work on your Steam Deck OLED yet. We should verify the actual SteamOS kernel, graphics stack and session requirements before choosing one.

## **Proposed `waydroid-box` acceptance criteria**

| Requirement | Target |
| ----- | ----- |
| Android runtime | Steam Deck |
| CPU and RAM workload | Steam Deck |
| Android GPU rendering | Steam Deck, hardware acceleration if supported |
| Display and input | Linux Mint PC |
| Launch and stop | Manual commands through SSH |
| Network | Existing LAN |
| Startup at boot | Disabled |
| SteamOS modifications | Minimize; inspect requirements first |

One caveat: video encoding on the Deck and decoding on the PC will also consume resources. The PC will never be completely idle, but Android application processing and rendering should remain on the Deck if we choose the architecture correctly.

## **My recommendation**

Proceed with a read-only feasibility audit before creating or installing anything. The key question is whether Waydroid can run reliably on the SteamOS host and expose its graphical output to a remote client without requiring an unsupported host modification.

One question before we plan the first test:

What do you want to see on the Linux Mint PC?

Only the Android screen or app, in its own window  
The entire Steam Deck desktop, including Waydroid

Should Waydroid run while the Deck is in desktop mode?

Yes — use the existing Steam Deck desktop session  
No — prefer a dedicated session that doesn't require the Deck's desktop to be open  
Continue feasibility audit

For the proposed waydroid-box project, display preference: Android screen/app in its own PC window; execution preference: use the existing Steam Deck desktop session. Continue with a read-only feasibility audit, no installation or modifications.

## **Read-only audit: preliminary findings**

The official documentation confirms three important facts:

1. Waydroid shares the host Linux kernel and uses Linux namespaces. It is a container-based Android environment, not a separate Android computer.  
    waydroid/docs · GitHub+1  
2. Waydroid's graphical session connects to a Wayland compositor. The documented startup sequence separates container startup from the user-level graphical session.  
    GitHub+1  
3. `scrcpy` is not automatically the answer. It is designed to mirror and control Android devices through Android Debug Bridge (ADB), but Waydroid is an Android container on the Deck, not a separate physical Android device. We'd need to verify whether its ADB interface and display capture support the workflow you want.  
    GitHub+1

The main unknown is therefore not whether the Deck can perform the computation. It is how to deliver Waydroid's graphics into a separate Linux Mint window while keeping the existing Deck desktop session intact.

## **Phase 1 — Inspect the existing Steam Deck session**

No installation, file changes, service changes or project creation yet.

Run this read-only command card in your existing Steam Deck host SSH terminal. It checks the session, graphics devices and whether relevant tools are already present.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

Inspection only • No sudo required

Copy inspection commands

Paste the complete output here. These checks do not install or start anything.

### **What happens next?**

* Phase 1: establish the Deck's actual graphics and desktop-session conditions.  
* Phase 2: determine whether an individual Waydroid window can be streamed to Linux Mint with acceptable performance.  
* Phase 3: assess GPU acceleration, input forwarding, security and the minimum necessary changes.

The project proceeds only if the architecture satisfies your requirements. We will not create `waydroid-box` or modify SteamOS before the feasibility audit supports doing so.

## **Phase 1 — Initial results**

Hardware detected  
SSH is headless

Your output gives us a useful starting point.

| Check | Result | Interpretation |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| SteamOS | holo / Arch-based | Confirmed |
| Kernel | 7.2.7 Valve | Confirmed |
| GPU device | `/dev/dri/card0` | Present |
| GPU render node | `/dev/dri/renderD128` | Present |
| SSH graphical session | TTY | No graphical environment attached |
| Wayland display | Unset | Expected in SSH |
| Waydroid | Not installed | Clean starting point |
| Streaming tools | Not installed | No existing solution |

The important discovery: your SSH terminal is not the Steam Deck desktop session.

That does not mean the project is impossible. Your `loginctl` output shows an existing `seat0` session on `tty1`, which is exactly the session we need to investigate.

We should not launch Waydroid directly from the SSH environment with the expectation that it will automatically appear on the Deck desktop.

### **Phase 1 — Next inspection: identify the actual graphical session**

We need to distinguish between Steam Deck Game Mode (Gamescope) and Desktop Mode (KDE Plasma/Wayland).

This matters because Waydroid's graphical integration needs a compatible compositor. We want to reuse the existing desktop session, not create an unnecessary second graphical server.

Run this second read-only card:

STEAM DECK — host SSH

Read-only • Session and process inspection

Copy inspection commands

One practical detail: run this while the Deck is in Desktop Mode, if possible. We want to inspect the session you intend to use.

Once we have the result, we can evaluate whether an individual Android window can be exported to Mint without streaming the entire Deck desktop.

| Compositor | `kwin_wayland` | Active |
| Wayland socket | `/run/user/1000/wayland-0` | Present |
| User runtime | `/run/user/1000` | Active |
| User systemd | `default.target` | Active |
| GPU access | `/dev/dri/renderD128` | Present |

The most important finding is:

Your SSH session can potentially launch a graphical application inside the already-running KDE session by explicitly targeting its Wayland socket.

That is fundamentally different from starting a new graphical environment.

We can preserve the existing desktop, Steam session and compositor.

That matches your intended PC experience.

However, we must distinguish two things:

A possible streaming component is Sunshine, which documents KDE/KWin Wayland capture and AMD VA-API encoding. Whether its capture can meet our individual-window requirement needs further validation.

## **Waydroid feasibility audit — Binder result**

This is the first significant compatibility finding, and unfortunately it is a serious blocker.

Your Steam Deck kernel reports:

```
# CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC is not set
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_DEVICES=""
```

And the device checks found:

* No `/dev/binder*`, `/dev/anbox-binder*` or `/dev/vndbinder*` devices.  
* No Binder or Ashmem modules currently loaded.  
* Your user is `deck`, UID/GID `1000`, with `wheel` membership. No special Waydroid permissions are currently present.

Important nuance: `CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y` means the kernel has the Rust Binder IPC implementation enabled, even though the traditional Binder IPC option is disabled. So we should not conclude that Binder is entirely absent just from the first line. However, the empty `CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_DEVICES` setting and missing device nodes mean we have not established that Waydroid can access the Binder interfaces it needs.

The graphics check was interrupted by a missing `fi` in my command card. That was my mistake; it did not modify anything.

## **A more precise finding: the kernel has Rust Binder**

I checked the exact configuration against the Waydroid project's issue tracker. There is a reported compatibility problem involving the same three kernel settings you have:

```
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y
# CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC is not set
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_DEVICES=""
```

The report describes Waydroid driver detection and Binder device setup problems on newer Arch-based kernels. It is particularly relevant because your SteamOS kernel is also in the Linux 7.2 family. The issue was closed, but the page still contains a September 2026 report describing a workaround; that is not proof of a clean, officially supported solution.

GitHub+1

So I would revise the assessment:

* Binder support: present in the kernel through the Rust implementation.  
* Binder device access: not currently established; no device nodes were found.  
* Waydroid compatibility: uncertain, with a directly relevant upstream issue.  
* SteamOS changes: not justified by this audit. The workaround described online involves persistent host-level changes, which I would not recommend adopting without much more scrutiny.

This is not yet a definitive rejection, but it is a meaningful warning.

## **Waydroid audit — updated findings**

This is a better result than the missing device nodes initially suggested.

Your kernel has the Rust Binder driver and Binder filesystem support:

```
nodev binder
rust_binder: present
```

That means Binder support is compiled into the running kernel and the Binder filesystem is registered. However:

* BinderFS is not mounted.  
* `/dev/binderfs` does not exist.  
* No Binder device nodes are currently exposed.

This distinction matters: we have evidence that the kernel can support BinderFS, but we have not yet demonstrated that Waydroid can initialise and use it on SteamOS.

Your graphics driver is also confirmed:

```
driver: amdgpu
```

That is the AMD GPU driver used by the Steam Deck. The installed `glxinfo`, `eglinfo` and `vulkaninfo` utilities give us ways to inspect the graphics stack without installing anything.

Current assessment: the kernel has a promising Binder foundation, and the Deck's AMD graphics stack is present. The missing piece is whether the Binder implementation and Waydroid's container setup work together on this SteamOS build.

It describes a system with almost exactly your kernel configuration. The Rust Binder driver can expose Waydroid-compatible devices, but the required device setup depends on the kernel's Binder parameters. The report also identifies a separate Rust Binder stability problem under real Android workloads.

## **Waydroid audit — Binder parameter finding**

This output confirms another important detail.

```
/sys/module/rust_binder/parameters/rust_devices
rust_devices:
```

The `rust_devices` parameter exists, but its value is empty.

Combined with the earlier results:

* Rust Binder is compiled into and active in the kernel.  
* BinderFS is registered.  
* The Rust Binder device list is empty.  
* No Binder devices are exposed.  
* No BinderFS mount exists.  
* Your kernel command line contains no Binder-related parameters.

This suggests the kernel has the necessary Binder infrastructure, but it has not been configured to expose the device interfaces Waydroid needs.

The GPU identification is also conclusive:

```
vendor: 0x1002
device: 0x1435
driver: amdgpu
```

That is the AMD Van Gogh APU platform used by the Steam Deck. The Deck has the correct general GPU hardware for hardware-accelerated Android graphics, subject to the Waydroid/Mesa integration working correctly.

### **One important correction to our interpretation**

The empty `rust_devices` value does not necessarily mean Waydroid cannot use Binder.

The Linux kernel source shows that this parameter specifies which Binder devices are created automatically when BinderFS is mounted. With an empty value, those default devices are not created. BinderFS can still provide a `binder-control` interface through which devices can be created dynamically.

Git at Google+1

That gives us two distinct possibilities:

1. Waydroid may be able to create its Binder devices dynamically using BinderFS.  
2. Waydroid's current setup may expect pre-created devices and require additional configuration.

We need to establish which applies to the current Waydroid release before drawing conclusions.

| ----- | ----- |
| SSH-triggered launch | Technically feasible |
| Existing Deck KDE session | Confirmed |
| Android CPU workload on Deck | Architecture supports it |
| Android GPU workload on Deck | Hardware suitable |
| Binder integration | Not yet verified |
| Individual Android windows on Mint | Not yet verified |
| Remote input and display | Not yet verified |

One clarification about your original requirement: Mint will still use some CPU/GPU resources to decode and display the remote video. The Android application itself, its CPU execution, and its GPU rendering can remain on the Deck.

My recommendation: don't create `waydroid-box` yet. Let's investigate the current upstream Waydroid BinderFS setup and its compatibility with Rust Binder first. That is now the most important unresolved technical question.

I found something particularly important for this project: Valve appears to have reintroduced traditional Android Binder IPC in SteamOS 3.9.3 Beta.

Your current kernel configuration is from the problematic generation:

```
# CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC is not set
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y
```

A report dated October 19, 2026 says Valve's October 16 SteamOS 3.9.3 Beta notes include:

> Reintroduce support for Android binder IPC

The report also says the change had not yet been independently verified as a working Waydroid setup.

mixed-news.com

This creates two possible paths:

1. Current SteamOS kernel: investigate the Rust Binder compatibility problems.  
2. SteamOS 3.9.3 Beta: investigate whether Valve's restored traditional Binder support makes Waydroid viable without third-party kernel modules.

I would prefer the second path if it proves reliable, but I would not recommend changing your SteamOS update channel just to test it.

## **Investigation: the Binder situation is more nuanced than it first appeared**

I inspected the actual Waydroid source code, not just forum instructions, and found that its current Binder setup already has a BinderFS path.

The relevant source is [`tools/helpers/drivers.py`](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/main/tools/helpers/drivers.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

Its setup logic:

1. Detects whether BinderFS is registered in `/proc/filesystems`.  
2. If it is available, creates `/dev/binderfs` and mounts BinderFS.  
3. Allocates the three Binder devices through `binder-control`.  
4. Creates device links under `/dev`.  
5. Checks that the Binder, vendor Binder and hardware Binder devices exist.

This is significant: Waydroid does not necessarily need the traditional C Binder driver if its BinderFS path works with the kernel's Rust implementation.

Git at Google+2

Your Deck has already passed the first prerequisite: BinderFS is registered. We have not tested mounting it or allocating devices, because that would modify the running system.

### **The Rust Binder crash has a known fix — but there is another unresolved issue**

I examined two separate Waydroid reports. They should not be confused:

| Problem | Investigation result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Rust Binder crash following a `bindgen` 0.73.1 rebuild | Fix identified |
| Rust Binder failing large transactions with `ENOSPC` | Unresolved report |

The first report documents a kernel crash in `rust_binder_open()`. Testing confirmed that rebuilding with `bindgen 0.73.2` fixed it, and Arch subsequently shipped a corrected kernel.

GitHub

The second report concerns transactions of approximately 1 MB or larger, which can cause Android services to abort and the container to crash. It was still open in the latest issue information I inspected, and its proposed fix was disputed in a later comment.

GitHub

This matters because your kernel is in the Linux 7.2 family. We should check its build configuration rather than assume it contains the same Rust Binder fixes as Arch's kernel.

### **Remote display: a better candidate than Sunshine**

I also investigated the Mint-to-Deck display side.

I think scrcpy is the more promising solution for your requirement.

Mirrors an Android screen in its own Linux application window, with keyboard and mouse control. Supports TCP/IP connections and does not require root on Android.

GitHub+1

Waydroid also documents ADB connectivity through its Android IP address and port `5555`.

GitHub

The proposed architecture would be:

Steam Deck OLED

Existing KDE Wayland session

Waydroid container

Android CPU \+ GPU rendering

ADB interface

SSH-encrypted tunnel

Linux Mint PC

scrcpy window

Android image \+ keyboard/mouse input

The ADB connection should be tunnelled through SSH rather than exposing an unencrypted ADB connection to the LAN. scrcpy's own documentation explicitly warns that remote ADB-server communications are unencrypted.

GitHub

One distinction: this would give you the Android display in one independent Mint window. It does not yet establish that every Android app can appear as a separate, independent Mint window.

Let's continue with Waydroid SSH as the primary design. I’ll keep this investigation read-only and focus on proving the two critical parts independently:

1. Can your Steam Deck kernel provide the Binder devices Waydroid needs?  
2. Can Linux Mint and Windows securely display and control the Deck's Android session through SSH?

The goal is to establish a technically sound architecture before we touch the Deck.

## **Finding 1 — the remote display design is now well supported**

The official scrcpy documentation explicitly provides an SSH-tunnel mode, including commands for both Bash and Windows PowerShell. It also provides a variant using only local SSH port forwards, avoiding remote port forwarding. That is a good fit for your existing LAN-only SSH setup.

GitHub+1

One correction to the earlier conceptual diagram: scrcpy's tunnel is not simply “ADB port 5555 through SSH.” The documented design tunnels the ADB server connection and scrcpy's video/control forwarding ports. We should follow that documented arrangement rather than expose ADB directly on the LAN.

## **Finding 2 — Rust Binder has a real Waydroid stability risk**

I found a more important compatibility issue in the Waydroid project itself.

A Waydroid report describes failures with the Rust Binder driver (`CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y`): large Binder transactions can fail with `ENOSPC`, causing Android services to abort and the session to crash. This was reported on Linux 7.2.2 with Waydroid 1.6.3. It is a user-reported issue, not proof that your exact SteamOS kernel has the same defect.

GitHub

That means our test must establish more than whether BinderFS can create device nodes. We also need to assess whether Android remains stable under realistic workloads. I would not accept “Waydroid starts” as the success criterion.

## **Finding 3 — two separate Binder problems must not be confused**

I inspected the full discussion of two Waydroid reports.

| Issue | What happened | Relevance to your Deck |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| [Waydroid \#2411](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/issues/2411?utm_source=chatgpt.com) | Rust Binder crashed with a `bindgen 0.73.1` regression. | Your reported `bindgen 0.72.1` is not that exact regression. |
| [Waydroid \#2422](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/issues/2422?utm_source=chatgpt.com) | Rust Binder failed large transactions with `ENOSPC`. | A separate, unresolved stability concern. |

The first issue was fixed in affected Arch kernels by rebuilding with `bindgen 0.73.2`. The discussion specifically confirms that the `0.73.1` problem is unrelated to `0.72.1`. That is reassuring for your Deck, but it does not establish that every Rust Binder issue is resolved.

The second issue is more concerning: its discussion includes a technical objection to the proposed upstream fix, and the reporter's large-transaction failure remains unresolved in the discussion I inspected.

Important: I would not apply the BinderFS workaround posted in the comments. It changes kernel device setup, Waydroid configuration and system services. It is a community workaround, not an established SteamOS solution.

## **ADB and SSH: the practical connection design**

Waydroid's official documentation confirms that its Android environment provides an ADB connection over port 5555\. [Waydroid ADB instructions](https://github.com/waydroid/docs/blob/master/faq/using-adb-with-waydroid.md?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

Combining that with scrcpy's documented SSH tunnel gives us this proposed design:

Steam Deck — host

Waydroid

Android runtime · internal ADB connection

ADB server

Deck-local connection to Waydroid · port 5037

SSH tunnel

Encrypted transport · forwarded ports · no public ADB exposure

Mint

ADB client \+ scrcpy

Windows

ADB client \+ scrcpy

The key advantage is that the Android ADB connection stays on the Deck. The remote machines communicate with the Deck's ADB server through SSH. scrcpy then provides the Android screen in a normal desktop window, with keyboard and mouse control. Its official documentation includes the required Windows PowerShell and Linux shell examples.

GitHub+1

We should now inspect the exact Waydroid code path for:

1. BinderFS creation and device allocation.  
2. How the Binder device names are selected and passed into LXC.

Then we can produce a Waydroid SSH technical feasibility report with a clear pass/fail/unknown matrix and a proposed design for both client operating systems.

My recommendation remains: don't abandon Waydroid yet. The remote side is technically credible. The main unresolved risk is now the interaction between Waydroid's BinderFS handling and SteamOS's Rust Binder implementation.

start the inspections

## **Inspection A — BinderFS startup and LXC device handling**

I inspected the current upstream Waydroid source, including `drivers.py`, `lxc.py` and `container_manager.py` (Waydroid version declared in its source: 1.6.3).

There are three important details.

### **A1. Waydroid does attempt BinderFS automatically**

Its startup sequence is:

1. Check whether the expected Binder device nodes already exist.  
2. If not, try `modprobe binder_linux`.  
3. Check whether the Binder filesystem is registered.  
4. Create `/dev/binderfs` and attempt to mount it.  
5. Allocate Binder devices using `binder-control`.  
6. Create links under `/dev/`.  
7. Pass those device nodes into the Android LXC container.

This is encouraging: Waydroid already contains a BinderFS setup mechanism. We may not need a separate custom BinderFS service.

### **A2. There is a genuine weakness in the current implementation**

The source uses these preferred Binder names:

```
anbox-binder
anbox-vndbinder
anbox-hwbinder
```

It then creates symbolic links with `ln -s`, without first cleaning up or safely replacing stale links.

That matches the failure scenario reported in Waydroid issue \#2422: after a reboot, a BinderFS mount disappears, but old links can remain and interfere with startup. The report is community-supplied, but the relevant code path is present in upstream source.

For our project: we should not assume that BinderFS working once means it will survive reboot correctly. Startup and reboot recovery need separate verification.

### **A3. Waydroid stores the selected device names during initialization**

The initializer calls `setupBinderNodes()` and saves the chosen Binder names in its configuration. Later, the container manager reloads those saved names and uses them to build the LXC device mounts.

This creates an important dependency:

Initialization

Detect Binder nodes → save device names → generate LXC configuration

Every subsequent startup

Recreate/check BinderFS nodes → reuse saved names → mount into Android

Potential failure

Saved name no longer matches an available device after reboot.

That is why I want the eventual installation procedure to include a reboot-recovery test, not just an initial launch test.

## **Inspection B — native KDE session versus SSH launch**

This is the most important discovery for your two-script design.

Waydroid's session manager reads its graphical-session environment when it starts. It expects:

* `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`  
* `WAYLAND_DISPLAY`  
* An existing Wayland socket  
* A usable D-Bus session

If `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` is missing, Waydroid defaults to `wayland-0`. If `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` is missing, it exits. It also checks that the Wayland socket actually exists.

It subsequently creates LXC mounts for the host Wayland socket and PulseAudio socket. [Waydroid session\_manager.py](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/main/tools/actions/session_manager.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com) · [LXC session mount implementation](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/main/tools/helpers/lxc.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

This has a direct consequence:

Native Deck launch

Naturally compatible

Launched from the existing KDE desktop, the script inherits the graphical-session environment.

SSH launch

Needs session integration

An ordinary SSH shell is headless. The script must deliberately connect to the existing KDE user session rather than start a separate graphical session.

This does not invalidate remote mode. It means `waydroid_ssh_start.sh` must be designed as a session-aware launcher.

I would investigate importing the existing user-session environment rather than creating another compositor such as Weston or Cage. That preserves your existing KDE desktop and avoids unnecessary complexity.

## **Inspection C — ADB and scrcpy transport**

I inspected Waydroid's actual ADB helper.

It does the following:

1. Starts the host ADB server.  
2. Reads the Android container's IP from its DHCP lease.  
3. Connects ADB to that IP.

It does not explicitly expose the ADB server on the LAN. [Waydroid net.py](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/main/tools/helpers/net.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

The scrcpy project documents a particularly suitable SSH arrangement:

Preferred: local port forwarding only

Conceptual example — not a command to execute yet.

```

ssh -CN \
  -L 5038:localhost:5037 \
  -L 27183:localhost:27183 \
  deck@192.168.1.161
 
```

On the remote client, point ADB at the forwarded server and use scrcpy's `--force-adb-forward` option.

```

export ADB_SERVER_SOCKET=tcp:localhost:5038
scrcpy --force-adb-forward
 
```

For Windows, the same environment variable and scrcpy option are supported in PowerShell.

This is based on scrcpy's official [SSH tunnelling documentation](https://github.com/Genymobile/scrcpy/blob/master/doc/tunnels.md?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

The second arrangement is preferable to exposing ADB on all network interfaces or enabling SSH remote port forwarding. It keeps the transport inside the SSH connection.

We will also need compatible ADB protocol versions on the Deck and client machines.

Strong architectural match

## **Inspection D — host networking side effects**

I inspected Waydroid's network startup and shutdown script.

This is worth highlighting because the Android runtime does more than create a container.

When Waydroid starts

* Creates the `waydroid0` network bridge.  
* Sets IPv4 forwarding to `1`.  
* Adds firewall rules through iptables or nftables.  
* Starts a dedicated dnsmasq DHCP/DNS service.  
* Uses the `192.168.240.0/24` container network.  
* Configures NAT for Android network access.

When Waydroid stops

* Removes its firewall rules.  
* Stops its dnsmasq process.  
* Removes the bridge.

One cleanup gap

The upstream shutdown script does not restore the previous value of `net.ipv4.ip_forward`.

Source: [Waydroid network script](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/main/data/scripts/waydroid-net.sh?utm_source=chatgpt.com).

This is a runtime host-network setting that can remain enabled after Waydroid stops, until something else changes it or the machine reboots.

For our installation design, I would require recording its original value and having an explicit, verified restoration procedure. We must also inspect the interaction with your existing Pi-hole setup before enabling this networking.

## **Consolidated inspection results**

Upstream source inspected: Waydroid main, version 1.6.3, commit `db7294c4c2bd43157bab94cf8a3015e22d9f1ad8`.

| Area | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| BinderFS automatic setup | Implemented |
| Rust Binder compatibility | Not proven |
| Stale Binder links after reboot | Identified weakness |
| Binder device mounts into LXC | Implemented |
| Existing KDE session integration | Possible, needs SSH environment handling |
| Android ADB connection | Implemented |
| SSH-only scrcpy transport | Officially documented |
| Mint and Windows clients | Supported by scrcpy |
| Host networking cleanup | Needs improvement |

## **Proposed final architecture**

## **Waydroid SSH**

Steam Deck OLED

One Android runtime · existing KDE session · AMD GPU

`waydroid_deck_start.sh`

Local

Uses the environment inherited from KDE.

`waydroid_ssh_start.sh`

Remote

Uses SSH and attaches to the existing KDE session environment.

SSH tunnel

ADB server \+ scrcpy video/control forwarding

Linux Mint

Android window

Windows

Android window

## **What remains before we can approve an installation**

I would not move to installation yet. Four areas need further inspection:

1. BinderFS reboot recovery: determine precisely how to prevent stale links and mismatched saved device names.  
2. KDE session environment: establish a reliable way for an SSH-launched process to use the existing graphical session and D-Bus.  
3. ADB authentication and isolation: establish how the Deck's ADB server will remain localhost-only, how client authorization works, and how the tunnel will be restricted.  
4. Network restoration: design a safe way to restore host forwarding and firewall state without interfering with Pi-hole or other services.

My verdict: the overall architecture is viable enough to keep investigating. The two-script design is sound, and scrcpy is a particularly good fit. The main engineering work is now making Waydroid's startup and shutdown reliable on SteamOS, especially BinderFS and the existing KDE session.

No changes have been made to the Steam Deck.

for reminder the Waydroid SSH is within a docker Distrobox, do not forget

Correct — that changes the architecture, and I’ll keep it explicit in the investigation.

Waydroid SSH runs from inside a Docker-backed Distrobox on the Steam Deck. The Deck remains the Android host in the broader sense, but Waydroid’s installation and control environment is the Distrobox, not an ordinary SteamOS host installation.

That means we must inspect two separate boundaries:

```
Linux Mint / Windows client
          |
       SSH tunnel
          |
Steam Deck — SteamOS host
          |
   Docker-backed Distrobox
          |
       Waydroid
          |
    Android container
```

The important question is whether the Distrobox can safely provide Waydroid with the Binder devices, LXC/container privileges, graphics devices, networking and existing KDE session access it needs — without granting it broad access to SteamOS or unrelated host data.

I’ll treat Distrobox → Waydroid as the primary execution boundary, and the host as a resource provider. I won’t assume that a host-side Waydroid installation is the intended design.

## **Inspection finding: Distrobox officially documents this architecture**

The Distrobox project has a dedicated section for Waydroid inside Distrobox. Its documented setup has several requirements:

* A rootful Distrobox with systemd.  
* `--unshare-all` when creating the Distrobox.  
* Host-level Wayland access.  
* Host-level Binder support.  
* LXC and networking support inside the Distrobox.  
* Correct host D-Bus and graphical-session environment variables.  
   GitHub+1

This is useful confirmation: the architecture is not inherently impossible or an unsupported experiment. However, the documented configuration is not automatically suitable for your security requirements.

## **The three boundaries we must verify**

1\. SteamOS host → Docker-backed Distrobox

Critical inspection

* Is the Distrobox rootful or rootless?  
* Which capabilities and devices are passed through?  
* Is the container using host networking or an isolated network?  
* Which host paths are mounted?

2\. Distrobox → Waydroid's LXC container

Critical inspection

* Can nested LXC create the required namespaces and cgroups?  
* Does the outer Docker container permit the required operations?  
* Can Binder devices be passed through without granting unnecessary host privileges?  
* Can Waydroid manage its own bridge and firewall rules without affecting Pi-hole or SteamOS networking?

3\. Distrobox → existing KDE session

Critical inspection

* Can the Distrobox access the existing Wayland socket?  
* Can it access the correct user D-Bus and audio socket?  
* Can SSH launch Waydroid through that existing session?  
* Can native Deck and remote SSH launchers control the same Android runtime?

## **Important Binder discrepancy**

The Distrobox documentation refers to the host's `binder_linux` module. Your Steam Deck kernel instead exposes the Rust Binder implementation, with BinderFS registered but no Binder devices currently allocated.

That is a real compatibility question. We must verify whether the Distrobox can see and use the host's Rust Binder devices, and whether Waydroid's automatic BinderFS setup works through this nested-container boundary. We must not install the suggested third-party DKMS module or modify the host to work around it.

GitHub+1

\-bash: docker: command not found  
(deck@Laco \~)\$

Excellent. This gives us two useful findings.

## **1\. Current Distrobox inventory**

Confirmed  
There is currently no Waydroid Distrobox.

| Distrobox | Image | State |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `pihole-box` | Arch Linux | Exited |
| `Stable-box` | Ubuntu 24.04 | Exited |
| `localai-box` | Ubuntu 24.04 | Exited |

We will leave all three untouched.

## **2\. Docker command unavailable**

`docker: command not found` tells us that Docker is not available through the current SteamOS host shell's PATH.

It does not prove Docker is absent: Distrobox may be using Podman, or Docker may be accessible through another environment.

We should identify the actual container engine before proceeding.

## **Next inspection — container engine**

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== CONTAINER ENGINE ==='

command -v podman
command -v docker

printf '%s\n' '=== PODMAN VERSION ==='
podman --version

printf '%s\n' '=== PODMAN CONTAINERS ==='
podman ps -a --no-trunc \
  --format 'table {{.Names}}\t{{.Status}}\t{{.Image}}'

printf '%s\n' '=== DISTROBOX ENGINE CONFIGURATION ==='
printf 'CONTAINER_MANAGER=%s\n' "${CONTAINER_MANAGER:-not set}"
```

All read-only.

Why this matters: I want to establish whether your intended architecture is actually:

```
SteamOS
  └── Podman
       └── Distrobox
            └── Waydroid
                 └── Android
```

or whether Docker is involved through a different layer.

We should not design the security boundary around an assumed container engine.

(deck@Laco \~)\$

\>   \--format 'table {{.Names}}\\t{{.Status}}\\t{{.Image}}'

pihole-box   Exited (143) 3 days ago  docker.io/library/archlinux:latest  
pihole       Up 20 hours              docker.io/pihole/pihole:latest  
Stable-box   Exited (143) 3 days ago  docker.io/library/ubuntu:24.04  
localai-box  Exited (143) 3 days ago  docker.io/library/ubuntu:24.04  
(deck@Laco \~)\$

Good. We have now confirmed the actual container engine.

Confirmed: Podman

Your current architecture is:

```
SteamOS host
  └── Podman 6.0.2
       ├── pihole-box       [stopped]
       ├── Stable-box       [stopped]
       ├── localai-box      [stopped]
       └── pihole           [running]
```

There is no Docker executable, and `CONTAINER_MANAGER` is unset. Distrobox is successfully using Podman through its default engine selection.

Important distinction: the fact that Distrobox uses Podman does not mean the Waydroid project should use Docker. We should design around Podman unless there is a compelling, verified reason otherwise.

Pi-hole is running, so networking and firewall inspections must account for it. We will not touch it.

## **Confirmed architecture**

```
SteamOS
  └── Podman 6.0.2
       ├── Distrobox containers
       └── Pi-hole container
```

One correction to our earlier terminology: Distrobox is running on Podman, not Docker. The Ubuntu and Arch images are container images; their Docker Hub origin does not mean Docker is installed or being used.

## **Next inspection — actual container security configuration**

I want to compare the three existing Distroboxes rather than assume they were created with identical settings.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== PODMAN ROOTLESS STATUS ==='
podman info --format 'Rootless={{.Host.Security.Rootless}}'

printf '%s\n' '=== DISTROBOX CONTAINER CONFIGURATIONS ==='
for box in pihole-box Stable-box localai-box; do
    printf '\n--- %s ---\n' "$box"

    podman inspect --format \
'User={{.Config.User}}
Privileged={{.HostConfig.Privileged}}
CapAdd={{json .HostConfig.CapAdd}}
SecurityOpt={{json .HostConfig.SecurityOpt}}
NetworkMode={{.HostConfig.NetworkMode}}
PidMode={{.HostConfig.PidMode}}
IpcMode={{.HostConfig.IpcMode}}
Devices={{json .HostConfig.Devices}}
Binds={{json .HostConfig.Binds}}
Mounts={{json .Mounts}}
RestartPolicy={{json .HostConfig.RestartPolicy}}
Labels={{json .Config.Labels}}' "$box"
done
```

This is strictly read-only. It deliberately excludes container environment variables, which could contain credentials.

What we're looking for: whether existing Distroboxes use rootless Podman, privileged mode, broad host mounts, host networking, extra capabilities or device passthrough.

That will tell us how much isolation is realistically available for a future Waydroid Distrobox — without touching Pi-hole or the existing projects.

## **Inspection results: important security finding**

This is a significant result. All three existing Distroboxes use rootful-style container configurations, even though Podman itself reports `Rootless=true`.

That distinction matters: rootless Podman does not automatically make a container safely isolated.

High exposure configuration

### **1\. Configuration comparison**

| Setting | Pi-hole box | Stable box | LocalAI box |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Podman rootless | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Container user | root | root | root |
| Privileged | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| AppArmor | Unconfined | Unconfined | Unconfined |
| SELinux label | Disabled | Disabled | Disabled |
| Host network | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Host PID namespace | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Host IPC namespace | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Host `/home/deck` RW | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Host `/` at `/run/host` RW | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Host `/dev` RW | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Automatic restart | No | No | No |

These are directly visible in the supplied inspection output.

Pasted text.txt

### **2\. Why this matters for Waydroid**

The current Distrobox defaults are deliberately permissive. They provide convenience and compatibility, but they are not the isolation boundary we want for a security-conscious Android runtime.

In particular:

* `Privileged=true` substantially weakens container isolation.  
* `apparmor=unconfined` removes an important confinement layer.  
* `unmask=all` exposes normally masked system paths.  
* Host PID and IPC namespaces remove namespace separation.  
* `/home/deck` mounted read-write exposes unrelated project files and potentially credentials.  
* `/` mounted read-write at `/run/host` exposes the host filesystem.  
* Host `/dev` access is broader than the Binder, GPU and input devices Waydroid actually needs.

This is not a recommendation to change your existing boxes. We are inspecting them, not correcting them.

### **3\. One important technical qualification**

There is a distinction I want to preserve:

`Rootless=true` means Podman runs without host-root privileges. It does not mean the containers are tightly sandboxed.

For example, `Privileged=true` in rootless Podman cannot simply grant unrestricted host-root authority. However, combined with the host namespaces and extensive read-write mounts shown here, it still gives the container processes broad access to the Deck user's environment.

So the accurate assessment is:

* Confirmed  
   Rootless Podman.  
* Confirmed  
   Highly permissive container configuration.  
* Not established  
   Exact effective host privilege and isolation boundaries.

## **Next inspection — effective user and namespace isolation**

I want to establish the actual UID mapping and remaining security restrictions. This is especially important before designing a separate Waydroid box.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== PODMAN USER NAMESPACE ==='
podman unshare cat /proc/self/uid_map
podman unshare cat /proc/self/gid_map

printf '%s\n' '=== CONTAINER SECURITY DETAILS ==='
for box in pihole-box Stable-box localai-box; do
    printf '\n--- %s ---\n' "$box"

    podman inspect --format \
'UsernsMode={{.HostConfig.UsernsMode}}
CgroupnsMode={{.HostConfig.CgroupnsMode}}
CapDrop={{json .HostConfig.CapDrop}}
ReadonlyRootfs={{.HostConfig.ReadonlyRootfs}}
AppArmorProfile={{.AppArmorProfile}}
ProcessLabel={{.ProcessLabel}}
MountLabel={{.MountLabel}}
MaskedPaths={{json .HostConfig.MaskedPaths}}
ReadonlyPaths={{json .HostConfig.ReadonlyPaths}}' "$box"
done
```

This is read-only; `podman unshare` enters Podman's existing user namespace to display its mappings. It does not start or modify any container.

### **What comes next**

After this, we can establish a security design for a dedicated Waydroid Distrobox, rather than copying the permissive settings of the existing boxes.

The objective remains:

```
SteamOS host
  └── Rootless Podman
       └── Dedicated Waydroid Distrobox
            └── Waydroid / LXC
                 └── Android
```

With only the specific host resources required by Android passed through — not blanket access to `/home/deck`, the host filesystem or all devices.

## **Results: user namespace mapping confirmed**

The mapping is:

```
Container UID 0  → SteamOS UID 1000 (deck)
Container UID 1  → host UID 100000
Container UID 2  → host UID 100001
...
Container UID 65536 → host UID 165535
```

The same mapping applies to GIDs.

Confirmed  
Podman is using a private user namespace for these containers (`UsernsMode=private`).

This is an important qualification to our previous security assessment: a process running as root inside these containers does not automatically run as root on SteamOS. Container root maps to your normal `deck` account.

However, the extensive host mounts and namespace sharing remain important exposure risks. Private UID mapping does not make a read-write mount of `/home/deck` or `/` harmless.

## **Why the second inspection partially failed**

The `CgroupnsMode` field isn't available in the Podman 6.0.2 inspection structure exposed by your installation.

Because the Go template stopped at that missing field, none of the fields after it were actually inspected. We should not treat them as empty or disabled.

Let's retrieve the remaining fields directly from Podman's JSON output instead. This avoids relying on a field that your version doesn't expose.

## **Next inspection — corrected command**

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
python3 -c '
import json, subprocess

fields = [
    "UsernsMode",
    "CapDrop",
    "ReadonlyRootfs",
    "MaskedPaths",
    "ReadonlyPaths",
]

for name in ("pihole-box", "Stable-box", "localai-box"):
    data = json.loads(subprocess.check_output(
        ["podman", "inspect", name], text=True
    ))[0]

    host = data.get("HostConfig", {})

    print(f"\n--- {name} ---")
    for field in fields:
        print(f"{field}={host.get(field, \"NOT EXPOSED\")}")

    for field in ("AppArmorProfile", "ProcessLabel", "MountLabel"):
        print(f"{field}={data.get(field, \"NOT EXPOSED\")}")
'
```

Read-only: it only retrieves container inspection metadata.

### **Where we stand**

| Security property | Finding |
| ----- | ----- |
| Podman rootless | Confirmed |
| Private user namespace | Confirmed |
| Container root maps to host `deck` UID | Confirmed |
| Privileged container setting | Enabled |
| Host filesystem exposure | Broad RW mounts |
| AppArmor confinement | Unconfined |
| Remaining capability restrictions | Not yet inspected |

The important security distinction is that container root maps to the ordinary Deck user, not host root. But because that user owns the home directory, a container with broad read-write home access can still reach sensitive user data.

We are getting closer to a realistic security design for the dedicated Waydroid Distrobox.

Excellent. This time we have a complete result, and it confirms several important details.

## **1\. Updated security assessment**

All three Distroboxes return identical values:

| Property | Result | Interpretation |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `UsernsMode` | `private` | Private user namespace |
| `CapDrop` | `[]` | No explicitly dropped capabilities |
| `ReadonlyRootfs` | `False` | Container root filesystem is writable |
| `MaskedPaths` | Not exposed | Unknown |
| `ReadonlyPaths` | Not exposed | Unknown |
| `AppArmorProfile` | Empty | No profile name reported |
| `ProcessLabel` | Empty | No process label reported |
| `MountLabel` | Empty | No mount label reported |

### **What this means**

1\. No explicit capability reduction

`CapDrop=[]` means Podman has not explicitly dropped capabilities through this setting. It does not tell us the exact effective capability set.

2\. Writable container filesystem

`ReadonlyRootfs=False` means the container's own root filesystem is writable. This is normal for many Distroboxes, but it is not a hardened configuration.

3\. No reported AppArmor profile

The empty profile name is consistent with the earlier `apparmor=unconfined` setting.

4\. SELinux labels

Empty process and mount labels are consistent with the earlier `label=disable` configuration.

5\. Masked and readonly paths

These fields are not exposed by this Podman inspection structure. We should not infer that no paths are masked or protected.

## **2\. Important distinction**

Our existing Distroboxes have this combination:

```
Rootless Podman
    +
Private user namespace
    +
Container root maps to deck UID 1000
    +
Privileged=true
    +
No explicit capability drops
    +
Host namespaces shared
    +
Broad host filesystem RW mounts
    +
AppArmor unconfined
    +
SELinux labels disabled
```

This is not equivalent to unrestricted host root, but it is a permissive environment with substantial access to the Deck user's resources.

We should not copy this configuration for Waydroid.

## **3\. Next investigation**

Before deciding whether Waydroid can operate inside a dedicated rootless Podman Distrobox, I want to resolve one architectural question:

Can we provide Waydroid with the host's Binder devices and graphical session access without giving the container broad access to the host filesystem and namespaces?

That is the key to designing a genuinely restricted environment.

The next phase should inspect the Distrobox Waydroid documentation and its exact requirements, especially:

* `--unshare-all` versus shared host namespaces.  
* Binder device access and BinderFS setup.  
* Wayland socket and D-Bus access.  
* LXC capabilities and nested container requirements.  
* Android networking and firewall requirements.

No changes to your Deck. We remain in the read-only feasibility phase.

lets continue

Let's continue with the next architectural question: what does the official Waydroid-in-Distrobox setup actually require, and which requirements can we satisfy with rootless Podman without opening up the Steam Deck host?

I'll keep the distinction clear between official documented requirements, what we've confirmed on your Deck, and what remains unproven. Still read-only; no installation or configuration changes.

## **1\. What the official Distrobox guide actually says**

I found the dedicated section, Using Waydroid inside a Distrobox, in the Distrobox project's documentation.

Its manual installation recipe explicitly calls for:

* A rootful Distrobox.  
* Systemd inside the Distrobox.  
* `--unshare-all`.  
* Host Wayland access.  
* The host `binder_linux` kernel module.  
* Additional packages including `kmod`, `iptables`, `libpam-systemd`, and `mutter`.

The automated recipe also uses a rootful Distrobox with `--unshare-all`.

GitHub

That is a significant finding, but we need to interpret it carefully.

## **2\. Rootful does not mean the same thing as container root**

Distrobox distinguishes these two modes:

| Mode | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- |
| Rootless Podman | Container root maps to an unprivileged host user |
| Rootful Podman (`--root`) | Container is created through the host's rootful Podman service |

The documentation explicitly explains that rootless container root is not real host root, while `--root` creates a rootful container.

GitHub+1

For your Deck, this matters enormously:

```
Current Podman:
rootless
  └── container root → host deck (UID 1000)

Documented Waydroid recipe:
rootful Podman
  └── container root → host root
```

I do not recommend switching to rootful Podman simply because the guide uses it. We first need to establish which specific operations require those privileges.

## **3\. The interesting part: `--unshare-all`**

The Distrobox guide also documents running nested LXC with `--unshare-all`, and says its LXC example works with rootless Podman as well as rootful Podman and Docker.

GitHub

This gives us an important lead:

Potential architecture to investigate

SteamOS host

KDE Wayland \+ Rust Binder support

Rootless Podman

Dedicated Waydroid Distrobox

Private namespaces

Nested LXC

Android runtime

But there is a catch: Distrobox's `--unshare-all` unshares devices/sysfs, IPC, network and process namespaces. It does not fully isolate the home directory or application access. It is not a complete security sandbox.

GitHub+1

That means we cannot blindly use the documented command either.

## **4\. A second mismatch: Binder**

The Distrobox guide says `binder_linux` must be present on the host. Your Deck is different:

* It has Rust Binder support compiled into the running kernel.  
* BinderFS is registered.  
* No Binder device nodes currently exist.  
* The traditional `binder_linux` module is not available.

So the documented recipe's Binder prerequisite does not exactly match your system. We cannot assume it works just because the kernel has Binder support.

The critical question is whether Waydroid can create and use BinderFS devices from inside a rootless, unshared Distrobox—or whether device creation must happen on the host with additional privileges.

## **5\. Good news: BinderFS itself supports user namespaces**

The Linux kernel documentation confirms that BinderFS can be mounted inside a user namespace. It creates a separate BinderFS instance with its own Binder devices.

The Linux Kernel documentation

This is a promising discovery.

It means we may not need to create Binder devices in the host's initial user namespace or expose a host BinderFS mount to the Distrobox.

Potentially:

```
SteamOS kernel — Rust Binder
          │
          ▼
Rootless Podman user namespace
          │
          ▼
Private BinderFS mount
          │
          ├── binder
          ├── vndbinder
          └── hwbinder
```

There is still an important condition: the process mounting BinderFS needs sufficient `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` in the user namespace governing its mount namespace. The kernel supports the operation, but we have not established that the proposed Podman configuration supplies the required capability.

Debian Manpages+1

## **6\. Updated feasibility matrix**

| Requirement | Status |
| ----- | ----- |
| Waydroid inside Distrobox | Documented |
| Nested LXC with rootless Podman | Documented |
| BinderFS inside user namespace | Kernel-supported |
| Rust Binder compatibility with Waydroid | Unverified |
| BinderFS mount with our intended Podman capabilities | Unverified |
| Wayland access with isolated namespaces | Needs testing |
| Android networking without host network sharing | Unverified |

## **7\. Next: inspect the nested LXC privilege requirements**

I want to compare two layers:

1. Capabilities required by Waydroid's LXC configuration.  
2. Capabilities available to root inside a rootless Podman Distrobox.

In particular, we need to establish whether `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, `CAP_MKNOD`, and `CAP_NET_ADMIN` can be provided safely within the private user namespace, without granting host-root authority or sharing the host's network namespace.

My current position: rootless Podman remains a viable avenue worth investigating. We have not proved that it can run the complete Waydroid stack, but BinderFS is no longer an immediate reason to reject it.

No changes have been made.

start the LXC inspection

Let's inspect the LXC privilege requirements first, before deciding how to configure the new Distrobox.

We'll separate three things that are easy to conflate:

1. Capabilities Waydroid asks LXC to retain for the Android container.  
2. Capabilities available to the outer rootless Podman container.  
3. Operations that actually require host-level privileges.

The first is already partly known from Waydroid's upstream LXC configuration. The next step is to inspect the exact configuration and distinguish required capabilities from inherited or optional ones.

## **1\. Upstream LXC configuration: exact capability list**

The current Waydroid `config_base` explicitly keeps the following capabilities for its Android LXC container:

GitHub+1

```
audit_control
sys_nice
wake_alarm
setpcap
setgid
setuid
sys_ptrace
sys_admin
block_suspend
sys_time
net_admin
net_raw
net_bind_service
kill
dac_override
dac_read_search
fsetid
mknod
syslog
chown
sys_resource
fowner
ipc_lock
sys_chroot
```

The list contains 24 entries, with `wake_alarm` repeated.

Important interpretation: `lxc.cap.keep` is an allow-list for capabilities inside the Android container. It does not mean the outer Podman container must automatically receive all these capabilities. We need to trace which operations require privileges in the outer environment and which are confined to LXC's own user namespace.

The official configuration also requests read-only cgroup and sys mounts, plus proc mounting.

GitHub

## **2\. What Waydroid actually passes into Android**

The upstream `lxc.py` generates a separate device-and-mount configuration. It attempts to pass through:

* The selected GPU render node.  
* Binder, vendor Binder and hardware Binder devices.  
* Standard device nodes such as `/dev/null`, `/dev/zero` and `/dev/fuse`.  
* `/dev/uhid` and `/dev/net/tun`.  
* Some optional hardware-specific devices.  
* `/sys/kernel/debug` and selected host system paths.

These are generated dynamically, and many entries are optional.

GitHub+1

Two details matter particularly for your Deck:

1. Waydroid expects Binder device paths to exist where it runs. Its LXC configuration then bind-mounts those devices into Android.  
2. It discovers and passes through the GPU render node, which is important because your Deck uses AMD Van Gogh graphics.

This means that even if BinderFS works inside the outer user namespace, we must still ensure the LXC device mounts can access those nodes.

## **3\. A critical security finding: the Android data mount**

Waydroid's session configuration bind-mounts its Android userdata directory into the LXC container.

GitHub+1

That is expected, but the exact source and mount propagation matter. A recent upstream report describes a serious filesystem-shutdown issue involving a bind-mounted Waydroid `/data` directory.

GitHub

For our design, this reinforces a rule:

Android userdata must live in a dedicated Waydroid-owned directory, never a broad host directory or a mount that exposes unrelated SteamOS filesystems.

## **4\. What the outer Podman container would need**

We must keep the privilege layers separate:

| Operation | Where privilege is needed | Main concern |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Create BinderFS instance | Outer Distrobox user/mount namespace | `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` in the relevant user namespace |
| Create Binder device nodes | BinderFS instance | Device creation and permissions |
| Start nested LXC | Outer Distrobox | Namespace and mount operations |
| Pass GPU/Binder devices | Outer Distrobox and LXC | Device access and mount permissions |
| Create Android network | Outer Distrobox network namespace | `CAP_NET_ADMIN` and veth support |
| Run Android | Inner LXC | Waydroid's retained capability list |

The upstream LXC manual confirms that `lxc.cap.keep` retains capabilities inside the LXC container and drops the others. It is not a request to grant those same capabilities to the host or outer Podman container.

Linux manual page

Preliminary conclusion: we should not give the outer Distrobox the full Waydroid capability list. We should identify the minimum outer capabilities separately.

## **5\. Next read-only check on your Deck**

Before testing nested namespace creation, let's inspect the host's current capability and user-namespace support.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== HOST PROCESS CAPABILITIES ==='
grep -E '^(Uid|Gid|CapInh|CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs):' /proc/self/status

printf '\n%s\n' '=== USER NAMESPACE LIMITS ==='
for file in \
    /proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces \
    /proc/sys/kernel/unprivileged_userns_clone
do
    if [ -r "$file" ]; then
        printf '%s = ' "$file"
        cat "$file"
    else
        printf '%s = not exposed\n' "$file"
    fi
done

printf '\n%s\n' '=== NESTED USER NAMESPACE TEST ==='
unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c '
    id
    printf "UID mapping:\n"
    cat /proc/self/uid_map
    printf "Capabilities:\n"
    grep -E "^(CapEff|CapBnd):" /proc/self/status
'
```

This creates only a temporary user namespace for the final test; it does not modify system configuration, mount filesystems, or start containers.

Once we have this output, we can assess whether the kernel permits the namespace nesting our design needs.

Not yet proven

Full Waydroid operation inside rootless Podman remains unverified. The most difficult outstanding points are nested LXC mounts, BinderFS access, and Android networking.

## **1\. Host capability results: an important distinction**

Your results are encouraging for rootless namespace support.

| Check | Result | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Host UID/GID | `1000` | Running as `deck` |
| Host effective capabilities | `0` | Ordinary user process has no effective Linux capabilities |
| Capability bounding set | `0x000001ffffffffff` | The kernel has not reduced the ordinary user's bounding set to zero |
| `NoNewPrivs` | `0` | No new-privileges restriction on this process |
| Maximum user namespaces | `59068` | User namespace creation is permitted by the configured limit |
| Unprivileged user namespace test | Successful | Your account can create a user namespace |

The test produced:

```
uid=0(root) gid=0(root)
UID mapping:
         0       1000          1

CapEff: 000001ffffffffff
```

This is exactly the expected distinction: inside the newly created user namespace, you appear as root and receive capabilities in that namespace, while remaining host UID 1000 with no effective host capabilities.

Confirmed

Unprivileged user namespace creation works on your Steam Deck.

## **2\. What this proves — and what it doesn't**

We have now established that:

* Rootless Podman has a working user-namespace foundation.  
* Nested user namespaces are permitted.  
* Namespace-local capabilities are available.

But this does not yet prove that we can:

* Mount BinderFS inside that namespace.  
* Run nested LXC successfully.  
* Create the required network interfaces.  
* Pass GPU and Binder devices into Android.

The large `CapBnd` value is also worth interpreting correctly: it is the bounding set, not the effective capabilities of your ordinary host shell.

## **3\. Next test: BinderFS mount inside a temporary namespace**

This is the most useful next experiment. We can attempt a BinderFS mount inside a temporary user and mount namespace, without mounting anything into the SteamOS host's mount namespace.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
unshare --user --map-root-user --mount sh -c '
    set -e

    printf "%s\n" "=== TEMPORARY NAMESPACE ==="
    id

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== PRIVATE MOUNT PROPAGATION ==="
    mount --make-rprivate /

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== TEMPORARY FILESYSTEM ==="
    mount -t tmpfs tmpfs /mnt
    mkdir /mnt/binderfs

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== BINDERFS MOUNT TEST ==="
    mount -t binder binder /mnt/binderfs

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== BINDERFS CONTENTS ==="
    ls -la /mnt/binderfs

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== BINDERFS MOUNT CONFIRMATION ==="
    findmnt /mnt/binderfs

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== TEST COMPLETE ==="
'
```

Host configuration unchanged

The temporary mount namespace disappears when the command exits. The test does not create Binder devices or change kernel settings.

```
unshare --user --map-root-user --mount sh -c '

    echo
    echo "=== TEST COMPLETE ==="
'
```

Important correction before we interpret results: BinderFS device creation is performed through an ioctl on `binder-control`, not by creating ordinary files or using `mknod`. This first command deliberately does not claim to create the devices; it confirms the control interface and feature directory in the isolated mount.
