Let's proceed. We can actually perform this test without GCC, Clang, additional packages or development headers: Python already installed on SteamOS can issue the Linux BinderFS ioctl directly.

We'll use Python's `fcntl.ioctl()` and the kernel's BinderFS device structure. This tests the real kernel interface, not a simulation.

## **BinderFS — actual device creation test**

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
unshare --user --map-root-user --mount sh <<'SH'
set -eu

echo "=== PREPARE PRIVATE NAMESPACE ==="
mount --make-rprivate /

echo
echo "=== CREATE TEMPORARY FILESYSTEM ==="
mount -t tmpfs tmpfs /tmp
mkdir /tmp/binderfs
mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs

echo
echo "=== CREATE BINDER DEVICES VIA IOCTL ==="

python3 - <<'PY'
import os
import fcntl
import struct
import stat

# Linux BinderFS:
# BINDER_CTL_ADD = _IOWR('b', 1, struct binderfs_device)
# struct binderfs_device = char name[256]; __u32 major; __u32 minor;

request = 0xC1086201
device_struct = "=256sII"
control = "/tmp/binderfs/binder-control"

devices = ("binder", "vndbinder", "hwbinder")

fd = os.open(control, os.O_RDWR)

try:
    for name in devices:
        print("\n--- " + name + " ---")

        buffer = bytearray(
            struct.pack(device_struct, name.encode() + b"\0", 0, 0)
        )

        fcntl.ioctl(fd, request, buffer, True)

        returned_name, major, minor = struct.unpack(
            device_struct, buffer
        )

        path = "/tmp/binderfs/" + name

        print("Returned name:", returned_name.split(b"\0", 1)[0].decode())
        print("Device number:", str(major) + ":" + str(minor))

        info = os.stat(path)

        print("Node exists:", os.path.exists(path))
        print("Character device:", stat.S_ISCHR(info.st_mode))
        print("Permissions:", oct(stat.S_IMODE(info.st_mode)))
        print("Owner UID:", info.st_uid)
        print("Owner GID:", info.st_gid)

finally:
    os.close(fd)

print("\n=== ALL DEVICE REQUESTS COMPLETED ===")
PY

echo
echo "=== BINDERFS DEVICE LIST ==="
ls -la /tmp/binderfs

echo
echo "=== TEST COMPLETE ==="
SH
```

### **What constitutes success?**

We want three actual character devices:

```
binder
vndbinder
hwbinder
```

Each should have a valid major/minor device number and exist inside the temporary BinderFS mount.

The namespace disappears when the command exits. No host `/dev` changes, persistent Binder devices, Podman modifications or package installations.

## **3\. An important remaining issue: device permissions**

The devices currently have mode `0600`.

That means only their owning namespace-local root can open them. We must investigate how Waydroid's initialization and LXC process access these devices, particularly because its upstream helper changes Binder device permissions.

We should not automatically copy Waydroid's `chmod 666` behaviour into our design. First we need to understand the actual UID/GID mapping and which process needs access.

## **1\. Inspect the upstream Binder implementation**

There are three operations to trace:

1. BinderFS initialization and device creation.  
2. Permission changes applied to the Binder device nodes.  
3. Device bind-mounts and access rules generated for LXC.

The key question is whether Waydroid's `chmod 666` is genuinely necessary for its intended execution model, or whether a more restrictive ownership and permission arrangement is possible.

## **1\. Host device access — confirmed**

Your output gives us a useful distinction between the GPU and Binder access models.

| Device | Owner | Permissions | Implication |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `/dev/dri/card0` | `root:video` | `0660` \+ ACL | Restricted graphics device |
| `/dev/dri/renderD128` | `root:render` | `0666` | Accessible to ordinary processes |
| `/dev/binderfs` | — | Absent | No persistent host BinderFS mount |
| `/dev/binder` | — | Absent | No traditional Binder node |
| `/dev/vndbinder` | — | Absent | No traditional vendor Binder node |
| `/dev/hwbinder` | — | Absent | No traditional hardware Binder node |

Your `deck` account belongs to neither `video` nor `render` according to `id`.

The render node is nevertheless world-accessible (`crw-rw-rw-`), so the absence of the `render` group membership does not prevent ordinary access to that particular node.

Good architectural news: we have no reason to expose the host's Binder device paths. Our successful BinderFS experiment supports creating a private BinderFS instance for the Waydroid environment.

## **2\. Upstream Waydroid permission handling — verified**

I inspected the actual upstream source at the previously recorded Waydroid commit `db7294c4c2bd43157bab94cf8a3015e22d9f1ad8`.

There is a significant detail:

* `drivers.py` creates BinderFS devices through the same `BINDER_CTL_ADD` ioctl we just tested.  
* `container_manager.py` explicitly changes the three Binder device permissions to `0666`.  
* `lxc.py` bind-mounts those devices into Android as `/dev/binder`, `/dev/vndbinder` and `/dev/hwbinder`.

So our test reproduces the same device-creation mechanism used by Waydroid.

GitHub+1

The permission change is deliberate: Waydroid makes the Binder devices accessible to processes that need them, rather than relying on namespace-local root ownership alone.

Security implication: `0666` permits every process that can reach those device nodes to open them. It does not expose them to the LAN, but it is broader than necessary if we can establish a narrower Android UID/GID access model.

## **3\. What the upstream source tells us about the security boundary**

There are two separate permission decisions:

A. Binder devices

Waydroid explicitly applies `chmod 666` to its three Binder devices before starting LXC. This is not an accidental side effect.

B. Other hardware devices

The same source has a general permission helper that can recursively apply `chmod 777` to selected graphics, media and hardware paths. It also has a separate path for Binder devices, using `666`.

This distinction matters: we should not assume every Waydroid device gets the same permissions.

The upstream implementation is available in:

* [Waydroid driver initialization](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/db7294c4c2bd43157bab94cf8a3015e22d9f1ad8/tools/helpers/drivers.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
* [Waydroid container manager](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/db7294c4c2bd43157bab94cf8a3015e22d9f1ad8/tools/actions/container_manager.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com)  
* [Waydroid LXC configuration](https://github.com/waydroid/waydroid/blob/db7294c4c2bd43157bab94cf8a3015e22d9f1ad8/tools/helpers/lxc.py?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## **4\. LXC's privilege requirements: another important finding**

Waydroid's base LXC configuration retains a broad capability list, including:

* `sys_admin`  
* `sys_ptrace`  
* `net_admin`  
* `mknod`  
* `dac_override`  
* `sys_chroot`  
* `sys_resource`

It also requests:

```
lxc.mount.auto = cgroup:ro sys:ro proc
```

And includes the dynamically generated device mounts. These are privileges and mount settings for the inner Android LXC container, not automatically privileges granted to the outer Podman container.

However, the nested arrangement still needs careful testing. We cannot assume all those operations will work merely because BinderFS works.

## **5\. Next investigation: SteamOS cgroup delegation**

LXC needs to manage its container processes and cgroups. Rootless Podman also needs an appropriate cgroup delegation arrangement.

Let's inspect the current host configuration without changing it.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== CGROUP FILESYSTEM ==='
findmnt -no TARGET,FSTYPE,OPTIONS /sys/fs/cgroup

printf '\n%s\n' '=== CGROUP VERSION ==='
stat -fc '%T' /sys/fs/cgroup

printf '\n%s\n' '=== CURRENT PROCESS CGROUP ==='
cat /proc/self/cgroup

printf '\n%s\n' '=== USER SYSTEMD DELEGATION ==='
systemctl show user@1000.service \
    -p ControlGroup \
    -p Delegate \
    -p DelegateControllers

printf '\n%s\n' '=== CGROUP CONTROLLERS ==='
if [ -r /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.controllers ]; then
    cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.controllers
else
    echo "cgroup.controllers unavailable"
fi

printf '\n%s\n' '=== CGROUP SUBTREE CONTROL ==='
if [ -r /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.subtree_control ]; then
    cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.subtree_control
else
    echo "cgroup.subtree_control unavailable"
fi
```

### **Why this check matters**

We need to establish whether LXC can receive a properly delegated cgroup subtree inside rootless Podman, rather than requiring access to the host's entire cgroup hierarchy.

That is one of the remaining major hurdles between a successful BinderFS test and a genuinely working, restricted Waydroid Distrobox.

No changes have been made.

## **6\. Cgroup investigation — results**

PASS

SteamOS is using unified cgroup v2.

Your output confirms:

| Check | Result | Interpretation |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Filesystem | `cgroup2` | Unified cgroup v2 |
| Mount options | `rw,...,nsdelegate` | Writable hierarchy with namespace delegation support |
| User service | `Delegate=yes` | User systemd service has delegation enabled |
| Delegated controllers | `cpu memory pids` | These three controllers are explicitly delegated |
| Available controllers | `cpuset cpu io memory hugetlb pids rdma misc dmem` | More controllers exist at the hierarchy level |
| Subtree control | `cpu memory pids dmem` | These controllers are enabled for child cgroups |

Your process is currently in:

```
/user.slice/user-1000.slice/session-24.scope
```

### **What this establishes**

This is encouraging for rootless Podman and nested LXC:

* cgroup v2 is available.  
* The user systemd service has delegation enabled.  
* CPU, memory and process-count controllers are delegated.  
* The hierarchy supports child cgroups.

But this is not yet a PASS for nested LXC.

The important distinction is that `Delegate=yes` on `user@1000.service` does not automatically mean the Podman container receives all the cgroup permissions LXC needs. We still need to inspect the actual rootless Podman container's cgroup namespace, mounts and writable subtree.

The `dmem` controller being enabled while absent from `DelegateControllers` is also worth noting, but I would not change anything based on that alone.

The `rintf` typo is harmless; the remaining commands executed successfully.

## **8\. Rootless Podman cgroup inspection — encouraging result**

PASS — cgroup visibility

Podman's own user namespace sees the same cgroup v2 hierarchy and enabled controllers as the host.

| Check | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Cgroup version | v2 |
| Cgroup manager | `systemd` |
| OCI runtime | `crun 1.28-dirty` |
| Network backend | `netavark` |
| Podman cgroup | `/user.slice/user-1000.slice/user@1000.service/user.slice/podman-306911.scope` |
| Visible controllers | `cpu memory pids dmem` enabled in subtree |

This is useful: Podman is running under the delegated user systemd hierarchy, rather than placing its processes in an unrelated or inaccessible cgroup tree.

Important limitation: `podman unshare` creates a user namespace, but it does not create a running container with the exact cgroup restrictions LXC would encounter. We have confirmed visibility and delegation at the Podman user-namespace level, not nested LXC's ability to create and manage child cgroups.

## **9\. Next hurdle: nested networking**

The cgroup results are encouraging, but networking may be more difficult than BinderFS.

Waydroid's upstream network script creates a dedicated `waydroid0` bridge, configures DHCP/DNS through `dnsmasq`, enables IPv4 forwarding and adds firewall/NAT rules.

Our Podman environment uses rootless Netavark. We need to establish whether a private network namespace can support the operations Waydroid expects, without touching the Deck's existing network or Pi-hole.

First, a read-only inventory.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
printf '%s\n' '=== NETWORK TOOL AVAILABILITY ==='
for tool in ip iptables ip6tables nft dnsmasq \
            slirp4netns pasta netavark; do
    if command -v "$tool" >/dev/null 2>&1; then
        printf '%-14s %s\n' "$tool" "$(command -v "$tool")"
    else
        printf '%-14s unavailable\n' "$tool"
    fi
done

printf '\n%s\n' '=== PODMAN NETWORK CONFIGURATION ==='
podman info --format \
'Backend={{.Host.NetworkBackend}}
Rootless={{.Host.Security.Rootless}}
NetworkCmd={{.Host.NetworkBackendInfo.Backend}}'

printf '\n%s\n' '=== HOST NETWORK FORWARDING ==='
sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward

printf '\n%s\n' '=== HOST NETWORK INTERFACES ==='
ip -brief link
```

After that, we can test bridge creation in a disposable network namespace. That test will not create a bridge on the host or change host forwarding.

Do not enable host IPv4 forwarding yet.

Waydroid's normal network script enables forwarding and creates firewall/NAT rules. We should first determine whether those operations can remain entirely inside the outer container's network namespace.

Also, the missing `dnsmasq` executable is not proof that Waydroid cannot work: it could be supplied inside the future Distrobox. But it is another dependency to account for.

## **Network architecture we want**

```
SteamOS host
  IPv4 forwarding = unchanged (0)
  Ethernet
     │
     ▼
Rootless Podman / private network namespace
  Waydroid Distrobox
     │
     ├── waydroid0 bridge
     ├── dnsmasq
     ├── private firewall / NAT
     │
     ▼
Android LXC container
     │
     ▼
Android applications
```

This is the desired isolation model, not yet a proven working configuration.

## **Next: test bridge capability without touching the host**

We can test whether an unprivileged user namespace can create a bridge and configure it with `ip`. The test uses a disposable network namespace and makes no persistent network changes.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
unshare --user --map-root-user --net sh -c '
    set -eu

    printf "%s\n" "=== PRIVATE NETWORK NAMESPACE ==="
    ip -brief link

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== BRIDGE CREATION TEST ==="
    ip link add waydroid-test type bridge

    ip link set waydroid-test up

    ip -brief link show waydroid-test

    printf "\n%s\n" "=== BRIDGE DELETION TEST ==="
    ip link delete waydroid-test

    printf "\n%s\n" "PASS: bridge created, enabled and deleted"
'
```

What this tells us:

* If it succeeds, the kernel permits bridge management in that user/network namespace.  
* If it fails with `Operation not permitted`, we have identified a privilege boundary to investigate.  
* Either way, the namespace disappears when the command exits.

This is still not a test of nested Podman networking or Android internet access. It isolates one specific prerequisite first.

## **What we have demonstrated**

Inside a disposable, unprivileged user \+ network namespace, you successfully:

1. Created a network namespace with `unshare`.  
2. Created the Linux bridge `waydroid-test`.  
3. Brought the bridge interface UP.  
4. Verified that it existed.  
5. Deleted it successfully.

The namespace then disappeared when the command exited.

No host network changes. In particular:

* Host IPv4 forwarding remains `0`.  
* No host bridge was created.  
* No firewall rules were added.  
* Pi-hole was untouched.

| Component | Status |

| GPU render-node access | PASS |
| Podman cgroup visibility | PASS |
| Private network namespace | PASS |

| Nested Podman \+ Waydroid networking | UNKNOWN |
| Android DHCP, DNS and NAT | UNKNOWN |
| KDE Wayland session handoff | UNKNOWN |

## **The important distinction**

We have established that the kernel allows an unprivileged namespace to manage its own bridge.

We have not established that rootless Podman gives a Distrobox the same network capabilities, or that Android LXC can operate successfully inside it.

The next useful investigation is the actual network namespace and capabilities available to a rootless Podman container. We should inspect Podman's existing configuration and runtime options before creating anything.

My assessment: the project is still technically plausible. BinderFS, GPU access, cgroup visibility and private bridge creation have all passed their isolated prerequisite tests. The difficult part now is proving that these capabilities survive the nested container boundaries.

good news 

next step ?

Good news indeed. We've now passed several important prerequisites without modifying SteamOS.

Next step: inspect the exact rootless Podman container configuration and capabilities.

The key question is whether a future Waydroid Distrobox can manage its own network stack and run nested LXC, without requiring host networking, host firewall changes or privileged access to the Deck.

We should investigate that boundary before creating any container.

### **Next test: private firewall, NAT and forwarding**

Bridge creation passed. Waydroid also needs firewall rules and IPv4 forwarding. Let's test those in the same disposable namespace.

This test will:

* Enable IPv4 forwarding inside the temporary namespace only.  
* Create a private bridge.  
* Create and populate a temporary NAT chain with `iptables`.  
* Remove the test chain and bridge.  
* Leave the namespace and all its changes behind when the command exits.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
unshare --user --map-root-user --net sh -c '
    set -eu

    echo "=== PRIVATE IPv4 FORWARDING ==="
    sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

    echo
    echo "=== PRIVATE BRIDGE ==="
    ip link add waydroid-test type bridge
    ip link set waydroid-test up
    ip -brief link show waydroid-test

    echo
    echo "=== PRIVATE NAT RULE TEST ==="
    iptables -t nat -N WAYDROID_TEST
    iptables -t nat -A WAYDROID_TEST -o lo -j MASQUERADE
    iptables -t nat -S WAYDROID_TEST

    echo
    echo "=== CLEANUP INSIDE TEMPORARY NAMESPACE ==="
    iptables -t nat -F WAYDROID_TEST
    iptables -t nat -X WAYDROID_TEST
    ip link delete waydroid-test

    echo
    echo "PASS: forwarding, bridge and NAT rule operations succeeded"
'
```

If this passes, we'll have demonstrated that an unprivileged network namespace can perform the three core operations Waydroid's network script needs.

Important: this still won't prove rootless Podman exposes those same operations to a Distrobox. That's the next boundary to investigate.

```

### **Why this is the right next step**

We need to distinguish two possible architectures:

A — Podman-managed networking

```
SteamOS
  └── Rootless Podman network
        └── Waydroid Distrobox
              └── Android LXC
```

Podman provides the outer connectivity. Waydroid manages its own private Android network inside.

B — Shared outer network

```
SteamOS
  └── Rootless Podman / pasta
        └── Distrobox network namespace
              └── Waydroid bridge + NAT
                    └── Android LXC
```

The Distrobox needs sufficient network capabilities to manage its own internal bridge and firewall.

My preference is B, provided it works without host networking, host forwarding changes or excessive privileges.

After this inspection, we can decide whether to authorize a disposable test container. No container creation is authorized yet.

## **13\. Next step: test the actual Podman boundary**

I recommend moving from theoretical inspection to a controlled test.

Proposed disposable test container

```
SteamOS
  │
  └── Rootless Podman
        │
        └── Disposable test container
              ├── User namespace
              ├── Private network namespace
              ├── Bridge creation
              ├── IPv4 forwarding
              └── Private NAT rules
```

Not Waydroid. No Android image, no Distrobox, no persistent storage, no host device mounts.

The test would answer whether the capabilities that passed with `unshare` also work through rootless Podman.

We would verify:

* Container UID/GID mapping and capabilities.  
* Whether `CAP_NET_ADMIN` is available.  
* Whether a private bridge can be created.  
* Whether private forwarding and NAT work.  
* Whether the host's `ip_forward` remains `0`.  
* Whether the test container can be removed cleanly.

One question before proceeding: do you authorize creating and then removing this disposable rootless Podman test container?

No existing containers, networks or projects will be modified.

One question before proceeding: do you authorize creating and then removing this disposable rootless Podman test container?

I authorize this test

clarify, is this podman inside a distrobox, or when you said rootless it means no distrbox needed correct, both approach are approved

Correct. Rootless does not mean Distrobox. It describes how Podman runs containers, not whether Distrobox is involved.

There are two distinct approaches:

| Approach | What it means |
| ----- | ----- |
| A — Direct rootless Podman | Podman creates and runs a container directly, without Distrobox. |
| B — Rootless Podman \+ Distrobox | Distrobox uses the same rootless Podman engine to create and manage its container. |

Both approaches are approved for investigation.

Our current test is Approach A: a disposable container created directly by rootless Podman. It is not a Distrobox.

This is deliberate: we first want to isolate what Podman itself permits, before adding Distrobox and its configuration layer.

## **Next: test the actual rootless Podman capability boundary**

Now we'll install the three required utilities inside the disposable Debian container, then test bridge, forwarding and NAT operations there.

This is the important difference from our earlier tests: the operations will run inside a real rootless Podman container, with only `NET_ADMIN` added.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
    --network=podman \
    --cap-drop=ALL \
    --cap-add=NET_ADMIN \
    --entrypoint /bin/sh \
    debian:12-slim -c '
        set -eu

        echo "=== INSTALL TEMPORARY TEST UTILITIES ==="
        apt-get update
        apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
            iproute2 iptables procps

        echo
        echo "=== CONTAINER CAPABILITIES ==="
        grep "^CapEff:" /proc/self/status

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE IPv4 FORWARDING ==="
        sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE BRIDGE TEST ==="
        ip link add wdtest0 type bridge
        ip link set wdtest0 up
        ip -brief link show wdtest0

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE NAT TEST ==="
        iptables -t nat -N WAYDROID_TEST
        iptables -t nat -A WAYDROID_TEST -o lo -j MASQUERADE
        iptables -t nat -S WAYDROID_TEST

        echo
        echo "=== CLEANUP ==="
        iptables -t nat -F WAYDROID_TEST
        iptables -t nat -X WAYDROID_TEST
        ip link delete wdtest0

        echo
        echo "PASS: rootless Podman network capability test"
    '

printf '\n%s\n' '=== HOST FORWARDING AFTER TEST ==='
sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward
```

The Debian package installation, test bridge and firewall chain all live inside the disposable container. No host mounts, privileged mode or persistent storage are used. The final host check verifies that SteamOS forwarding remains unchanged.

What we're looking for: a successful bridge, forwarding and NAT test, followed by `net.ipv4.ip_forward = 0` on the host. If any operation fails, send the complete output; don't try to fix it yet.

We found an important restriction. The test did not reach the bridge or NAT operations.

## **14\. Rootless Podman capability test — blocked during package installation**

BLOCKED — user namespace permissions

The failure is caused by Debian's APT privilege-dropping mechanism:

```
setgroups 65534 failed - Operation not permitted
setegid 65534 failed - Operation not permitted
seteuid 42 failed - Operation not permitted
```

APT tries to switch to its unprivileged `_apt` account while downloading packages. Our container was deliberately launched with:

```
--cap-drop=ALL
--cap-add=NET_ADMIN
```

That removes capabilities APT expects for its normal privilege transition.

This does not mean `NET_ADMIN` failed. We never reached that test.

### **What we have confirmed**

* The container was created and ran.  
* The package installation failed.  
* The temporary container was removed by `--rm`.  
* SteamOS IPv4 forwarding remains `0`.  
* No host configuration was changed.

## **Next step: separate package installation from the capability test**

We should install the tools in a temporary image first, then run a second container from that image with the restricted capabilities.

This avoids granting additional capabilities just to accommodate APT.

Proposed sequence:

```
Existing Debian image
       │
       ▼
Temporary image build
APT installs iproute2 + iptables + procps
       │
       ▼
Disposable rootless container
cap-drop=ALL
cap-add=NET_ADMIN
       │
       ▼
Bridge + forwarding + NAT test
       │
       ▼
Remove temporary image and container
```

This introduces a temporary local image, but no persistent project or host configuration.

I recommend this approach rather than weakening the capability restrictions. Do you authorize creating and removing this temporary test image as well?

proceed this approach is approved

Approved. We'll keep the strict capability restrictions and separate the two stages:

1. Build a temporary Debian image containing the networking utilities.  
2. Run it with only `NET_ADMIN`, test the network operations, then remove the temporary image.

No changes to SteamOS packages or existing Podman images.

    rm -rf "$tmpdir"
}

cat > "$tmpdir/Containerfile" <<'EOF'
FROM debian:12-slim

RUN apt-get update \
 && DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
      iproute2 iptables procps \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
EOF

printf '%s\n' '=== BUILD TEMPORARY TEST IMAGE ==='
podman build --pull=never \
    -t "$image" \
    -f "$tmpdir/Containerfile" \
    "$tmpdir"

build_rc=$?

if [ "$build_rc" -ne 0 ]; then
    echo "IMAGE BUILD FAILED"
    exit "$build_rc"
fi

printf '\n%s\n' '=== ROOTLESS PODMAN CAPABILITY TEST ==='

podman run --rm \
    --network=podman \
    --cap-drop=ALL \
    --cap-add=NET_ADMIN \
    --entrypoint /bin/sh \
    "$image" -c '
        set -eu

        echo "=== EFFECTIVE CAPABILITIES ==="
        grep "^CapEff:" /proc/self/status

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE IPv4 FORWARDING ==="
        sysctl -w net.ipv4.ip_forward=1

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE BRIDGE TEST ==="
        ip link add wdtest0 type bridge
        ip link set wdtest0 up
        ip -brief link show wdtest0

        echo
        echo "=== PRIVATE NAT TEST ==="
        iptables -t nat -N WAYDROID_TEST
        iptables -t nat -A WAYDROID_TEST -o lo -j MASQUERADE
        iptables -t nat -S WAYDROID_TEST

        echo
        echo "=== CLEANUP ==="
        iptables -t nat -F WAYDROID_TEST
        iptables -t nat -X WAYDROID_TEST
        ip link delete wdtest0

        echo
        echo "PASS: rootless Podman network capability test"
    '

test_rc=$?

printf '\n%s\n' '=== HOST FORWARDING AFTER TEST ==='
sysctl net.ipv4.ip_forward

printf '\n%s\n' '=== TEMPORARY IMAGE CLEANUP ==='
cleanup
trap - EXIT

exit "$test_rc"
```

### **What I want to see**

* Image builds successfully using the existing local Debian base.  
* `NET_ADMIN` is present in the effective capabilities.  
* Private forwarding succeeds.  
* Bridge creation succeeds.  
* NAT chain and MASQUERADE rule succeed.  
* Host forwarding remains `0`.  
* Temporary image is removed.

If any test fails, send the complete output. We won't broaden privileges or modify host networking to force a pass.

We found a significant limitation. The temporary image built successfully, but the actual network test stopped at the first forwarding operation.

## **15\. Rootless Podman network test — partial failure**

BLOCKED — IPv4 forwarding

### **Results**

| Test | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Temporary Debian image build | PASS |
| Install networking utilities | PASS |
| Run rootless Podman container | PASS |
| Drop all capabilities | PASS |
| Add `NET_ADMIN` only | PASS |
| Set private IPv4 forwarding | FAIL |
| Bridge creation | Not reached |
| NAT rules | Not reached |
| Host forwarding remains `0` | PASS |
| Temporary image cleanup | PASS |

The effective capability value was:

```
CapEff: 0000000000001000
```

That corresponds to `CAP_NET_ADMIN`, exactly as intended.

But:

```
sysctl: permission denied on key "net.ipv4.ip_forward"
```

### **What this actually tells us**

This is an important distinction:

* Our earlier direct user/network namespace test could change its own forwarding setting.  
* The rootless Podman container, despite receiving `NET_ADMIN`, could not change its network namespace's forwarding setting.

This suggests that Podman's namespace configuration or the runtime's sysctl restrictions impose an additional boundary.

It does not yet prove that bridge creation or NAT would fail. The script stopped before reaching them.

## **Next step: isolate the failure**

I recommend a second test that does not attempt to change `ip_forward`.

We'll test only:

1. Bridge creation and activation.  
2. NAT chain creation.  
3. MASQUERADE rule insertion.  
4. Cleanup.

Same restrictions: rootless Podman, `--cap-drop=ALL`, `--cap-add=NET_ADMIN`, no host mounts, no privileged mode.

However, the temporary image was removed as planned. Before repeating the test, we should rebuild it. We can make that build reusable for the next test and then remove it afterward.

My current assessment: the direct user namespace route is more capable than the network namespace Podman currently provides. We need to determine whether the remaining bridge and firewall operations work before deciding whether a Distrobox-based design is viable. No need to weaken security or enable host forwarding.

This is important context. I've reviewed the Pi-hole recovery script and the Stable Diffusion documentation. They give us two proven solutions on your Deck, and we should use them as architectural references rather than treating your Steam Deck as a generic Linux host.

## **What your existing projects actually demonstrate**

### **1\. Pi-hole — rootless Podman, low-port binding and firewall**

Your recovery script confirms this arrangement:

* Rootless Podman with `pasta`.  
* `net.ipv4.ip_unprivileged_port_start=53` to permit binding DNS port 53 without root.  
* Persistent `firewalld` rules restricted to `192.168.1.0/24`, TCP and UDP port 53\.  
* Existing container and systemd service are recovered rather than recreated.

Crucially, this is a host port-binding and inbound-firewall solution. It does not establish that a rootless container can configure its own bridge, enable IP forwarding or perform arbitrary NAT.

### **2\. Stable Diffusion — local WebUI, SSH tunnel**

Your documentation confirms:

* Stable-box runs Ubuntu 24.04 in Distrobox.  
* The WebUI listens only on `127.0.0.1:1234`.  
* Windows accesses it through an SSH tunnel.  
* No direct LAN exposure or firewall opening is needed.  
* The Deck handles the GPU work.

This is a particularly useful precedent for Waydroid's remote display access.

There are two separate networking questions:

1. Android networking: Can Android inside Waydroid obtain connectivity reliably without weakening the host or disrupting Pi-hole?  
2. Remote display: Can we transport the Android display over SSH, as we already do with Stable Diffusion, without exposing ADB or a display service to the LAN?

The Stable Diffusion solution is relevant to question 2, but it doesn't solve question 1\.

5. Keep native KDE Wayland and the existing SSH architecture.

One important distinction: Pi-hole's successful port 53 configuration doesn't prove that Waydroid's Android network can work with the same parameters. But it does prove that we already have a working, recoverable rootless Podman networking setup on this machine.

The files confirm a native host installation, with Waydroid integrated into SteamOS rather than containerized.

### **1\. The updater explains the post-update workflow**

`Waydroid-Updater.sh` is a small wrapper. It:

1. Changes to `/home/deck`.  
2. Deletes the previous `~/steamos-waydroid-installer` checkout.  
3. Clones the latest `ryanrudolfoba/steamos-waydroid-installer`.  
4. Makes the installer executable.  
5. Runs it.

So the updater does not merely update Android. It retrieves and reruns the upstream SteamOS installer, presumably to restore or update host integration after an OS update.

Important: it deletes the previous installer checkout before cloning. That is not the same as deleting the installed Waydroid data, but it does discard the previous installer source and any local modifications in that checkout.

### **2\. The launcher confirms the host-level integration**

Your Cage launcher calls host paths such as:

* `/usr/bin/waydroid`  
* `/usr/bin/waydroid-mount`  
* `/usr/bin/waydroid-firewall`  
* `/usr/bin/waydroid-startup-scripts`  
* `/usr/bin/waydroid-shutdown-scripts`

It also checks `waydroid-container.service`, adjusts the display through `wlr-randr`, and launches Android through Cage.

That is strong evidence of a host-native Waydroid installation. It also explains why the Steam shortcut could launch Android independently of the ordinary KDE desktop workflow.

### **3\. The Toolbox reveals what the installation changed**

The Toolbox exposes several host and Android configuration operations:

| Area | What the script changes |
| ----- | ----- |
| Network | firewalld interface, DNS/DHCP ports and forwarding |
| GPU | `waydroid_base.prop` gralloc setting |
| Audio | Android overlay `audio.rc` |
| Ad blocking | Android overlay `hosts` file |
| Service | Waydroid container service and session |
| Steam | adds the Cage launcher to Game Mode |
| Uninstall | removes packages, kernel module packages, system files and optionally Android data |

This is especially useful because it gives us a concrete list of host integration points to investigate.

## **Initial safety observations**

I found several things we should preserve as audit findings, not repeat blindly in a rebuild:

* Firewall handling: the Toolbox adds `waydroid0` to the `trusted` zone, makes forwarding and DNS/DHCP rules permanent, and then stops `firewalld`. That deserves particular scrutiny against your current SteamOS firewall configuration.  
* GPU permissions: one launcher variant contains commented-out suggestions to change permissions on `/dev/dri/card*` and `/dev/dri/renderD128`. We should not apply those broad permissions.  
* Uninstaller: the Toolbox has two uninstall paths. One removes Waydroid while intending to preserve Android user data; the other also removes Android data. Both delete host files and packages, disable SteamOS read-only protection temporarily, and remove configuration. Neither should be reused without a complete path-by-path audit.  
* Password handling: the Toolbox captures the sudo password in a shell variable and repeatedly pipes it to `sudo -S`. We should replace this pattern if we ever rebuild the Toolbox.  
* Launcher cleanup: it waits for any process named `cage` to exit before running Waydroid shutdown scripts. That is not a reliable way to identify the specific Cage instance it launched.

## **Waydroid — definitive project scope**

Mandatory architecture  
Waydroid inside a container

The Steam Deck is your project laboratory. Applications and services must live inside isolated environments such as Distrobox or Podman.

```
STEAM DECK OLED — SteamOS HOST
│
├── SteamOS
│   ├── KDE / Wayland
│   ├── AMD GPU
│   ├── Steam
│   └── SSH administration
│
├── Distrobox / Podman
│   ├── Pi-hole          [protected]
│   ├── Stable Diffusion [protected]
│   ├── LocalAI          [paused]
│   ├── Odysseus         [rebuild project]
│   └── Waydroid         [proposed project]
│       ├── Android runtime
│       ├── Android image and data
│       ├── Supporting services
│       └── Application integration
│
└── Host integration
    └── Minimum necessary access, explicitly audited
```

## **Role of the Ryan Rudolf project**

The Ryan project is reference material only.

We can learn from its:

* Installation and update procedures.  
* Binder integration.  
* Android image management.  
* Cage launcher and Steam integration.  
* GPU and audio configuration.  
* Network configuration.  
* Recovery and uninstall procedures.

But we are not trying to reproduce its native host installation.

In particular, we should not inherit its host-level package modifications, privileged operations, or destructive maintenance assumptions simply because they worked in the original setup.

## **What the audit is actually trying to establish**

| Question | Objective |
| ----- | ----- |
| Container feasibility | Can Waydroid operate inside Distrobox or Podman? |
| Binder | Can the SteamOS Rust BinderFS implementation be accessed safely? |
| GPU | Can Android use the AMD GPU without compromising host security? |
| Display | Can Waydroid integrate with KDE Wayland and Cage? |
| Networking | Can Android networking work without unsafe host-wide changes? |
| Persistence | Can Android data survive container recreation and SteamOS resets? |
| Recovery | Can the environment be rebuilt without reinstalling or modifying the host? |
| Isolation | Can we avoid privileged containers and broad host mounts? |

One important distinction: Distrobox is not a complete security boundary by itself. It integrates closely with the host. We must evaluate its actual permissions, mounts, capabilities and device access, not assume that putting something in a container automatically makes it secure.

## **The most important principle**

The host is infrastructure. The container is the application environment.

* SteamOS should remain as untouched as reasonably possible.  
* Waydroid installation and maintenance should be container-contained.  
* Persistent Android data should be independently recoverable.  
* Host integration should be minimal and documented.  
* No compromise of host security just to make Waydroid work.

## **And regarding the audit itself**

I completely agree with your final point.

A successful audit does not have to end with a successful installation.

There are three perfectly valid outcomes:

1. Feasible: a secure, maintainable container architecture is demonstrated.  
2. Conditionally feasible: it works, but requires specific compromises that you can evaluate.  
3. Not feasible: Binder, GPU, networking or another fundamental dependency prevents a sufficiently isolated implementation.

All three are useful results. Discovering that an architecture is unsafe or technically impossible before installing anything is a successful engineering outcome.

I will use this as the governing scope for the Waydroid project going forward: container-only, security-first, resilience after SteamOS reset. The native Ryan installation remains a reference, never the destination.

Option B — Rootless Distrobox

We should not assume either will work.

Inspect the exact requirements of Waydroid's LXC runtime and map each requirement to what rootless Podman can actually provide:

1. BinderFS mounting and device access.  
2. LXC nesting and required capabilities.  
3. Cgroups and namespaces.  
4. AMD GPU device access.  
5. Wayland socket access.  
6. Android networking.  
7. Persistent Android storage.

The result will be a requirement-by-requirement compatibility matrix, with each item marked verified, unverified, restricted or impossible.

My recommendation: start with rootless Podman as the baseline, then evaluate Distrobox against it. This avoids introducing unnecessary layers before we know whether the underlying container architecture is viable.

## **Phase 1A — source inspection findings**

The first important discovery is that Waydroid is not simply an Android application running inside a Linux container. It starts its own LXC system container, with its own namespaces, mounts and device access. The official project describes this architecture explicitly.

waydroid/docs · GitHub+1

That means our proposed architecture is actually nested:

```
SteamOS host
└── Rootless Podman container
    └── Waydroid service manager
        └── LXC Android container
            ├── Binder IPC
            ├── AMD GPU render device
            ├── Android system image
            └── Android data
```

This is the central technical challenge: we need LXC to work inside a rootless Podman container, not merely Waydroid to work on SteamOS.

### **1\. LXC permissions — major concern**

Waydroid's current base LXC configuration retains a broad capability set, including `sys_admin`, `net_admin`, `mknod`, `chown`, `sys_chroot` and others. It also configures cgroup, sysfs and proc mounts.

GitHub+1

That does not automatically mean we must grant all those capabilities to the outer Podman container. But it does mean we cannot assume a restrictive outer container will support the default Waydroid configuration unchanged.

Rootless Podman does not acquire host-root privileges: its container root is mapped through a user namespace.

GitHub  
Not yet proven  
Nested LXC may require operations that this boundary cannot safely provide.

### **2\. Binder — promising, but not proven in Podman**

Waydroid's current LXC configuration expects Binder devices to be available and bind-mounts them into Android. It also expects a graphics render device.

GitHub+1

Our previous Steam Deck test proved that:

* Rust BinderFS is available in the SteamOS kernel.  
* BinderFS can be mounted in a disposable user and mount namespace.  
* `binder`, `vndbinder` and `hwbinder` device nodes can be created.

But it did not prove that those operations work inside a rootless Podman container. That is the next test we need.

### **3\. GPU — good starting point**

Waydroid's LXC helper identifies the host render node and adds it to its device mounts. Your Deck already has an AMD render node.

GitHub+1

We should test access to only that render device, rather than passing all of `/dev/dri` or giving the container broad device access.

| Requirement | Assessment |
| ----- | ----- |
| SteamOS Rust BinderFS | Host verified |
| BinderFS inside rootless Podman | Untested |
| Nested LXC | High-risk uncertainty |
| AMD GPU render-node passthrough | Host device verified |
| Wayland integration | Host environment verified |
| Android networking | Unresolved |
| Persistent Android data | Design feasible |

The UID/GID mapping is particularly important:

```
Container UID 0       → Host UID 1000 (deck)
Container UID 1–65536 → Host UID 100000–165535
```

Container root is therefore not SteamOS root. However, files and devices explicitly exposed to the container can still create security risks.

## **3\. Next test: BinderFS inside rootless Podman**

We should test two distinct operations:

1. Can the container mount BinderFS?  
2. If mounted, can it create Binder devices using `BINDER_CTL_ADD`?

These are separate permissions. A successful mount alone does not establish that Android can use Binder.

### **Important security boundary**

The first experiment should use:

* Rootless Podman.  
* Debian 12 Slim.  
* No privileged mode.  
* No host network.  
* No host PID namespace.  
* No host BinderFS mount.  
* No broad device passthrough.  
* No host filesystem mounts.

We should initially test whether BinderFS can be mounted inside the container's own namespaces. This avoids exposing host Binder devices unnecessarily.

## **4\. One question before executing**

There is an important distinction between a disposable container and a disposable image.

The Debian image is already present, so we don't need to download anything. The experiment will create a temporary container and remove that specific container after recording its results. It will not remove the image.

Are you happy for me to proceed with this temporary rootless BinderFS experiment?

No persistent configuration changes will be made.

Debian approved

Approved. We'll use the existing Debian 12 Slim image for the first controlled test.

We'll start with the strictest baseline: zero Linux capabilities, no network, no host mounts and no privileged mode.

## **Test 1 — BinderFS mount under strict rootless isolation**

Temporary experiment only  
STEAM DECK — host SSH

Run this single command:

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binderfs-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CONTAINER IDENTITY ====="
    id

    echo
    echo "===== BINDER FILESYSTEM SUPPORT ====="
    grep binder /proc/filesystems || true

    echo
    echo "===== MOUNT COMMAND ====="
    command -v mount || true

    echo
    echo "===== BINDERFS MOUNT TEST ====="
    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs
    RESULT=$?

    echo "Mount exit code: $RESULT"

    if [ "$RESULT" -eq 0 ]; then
      echo
      echo "===== BINDERFS CONTENTS ====="
      ls -la /tmp/binderfs
      umount /tmp/binderfs
    fi

    exit 0
  '
```

### **What we're measuring**

* Whether Debian sees BinderFS support from the kernel.  
* Whether the `mount` utility is available.  
* Whether BinderFS can be mounted without granting any additional capabilities.

A mount failure is not necessarily a dead end. It tells us which restriction we encounter under the strict baseline.

Please paste the complete output. We'll interpret it before deciding whether a second test with narrowly scoped `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` is justified.

## **Test 1 — Results**

| Check | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Container identity | UID 0, mapped rootless |
| BinderFS kernel support | Visible |
| `mount` utility | Available |
| BinderFS mount | Denied |
| Exit code | `32` |

The important distinction:

* `nodev binder` in `/proc/filesystems` confirms the container can see the host kernel's BinderFS filesystem support.  
* `permission denied` confirms that visibility alone does not grant permission to mount it.  
* We have not yet tested Binder device creation or Binder transactions.

## **Next — Test 2: narrowly scoped capability**

The next experiment is to add only `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` to this disposable container. This is a significant capability, so we'll use it only for this isolated test, with no host mounts, devices, networking or privileged mode.

It will tell us whether the mount failure is simply the missing capability or whether another restriction (such as the user namespace or security policy) also blocks BinderFS.

Additional privilege — temporary test only

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binderfs-cap-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CAPABILITIES ====="
    grep "^Cap" /proc/self/status

    echo
    echo "===== BINDERFS SUPPORT ====="
    grep binder /proc/filesystems || true

    echo
    echo "===== BINDERFS MOUNT TEST ====="
    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs
    RESULT=$?

    echo "Mount exit code: $RESULT"

    if [ "$RESULT" -eq 0 ]; then
      echo
      echo "===== BINDERFS CONTENTS ====="
      ls -la /tmp/binderfs
      echo
      echo "===== BINDER CONTROL DEVICE ====="
      ls -l /tmp/binderfs/binder-control
      umount /tmp/binderfs
    fi

    exit 0
  '
```

This does not change host capabilities or configuration. The container is automatically removed on exit; the Debian image remains.

## **Test 2 — Results**

Mount successful

| Check | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Rootless Podman | Yes |
| Container UID | 0 (namespace-mapped root) |
| Capabilities | `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` only |
| BinderFS visible | Yes |

| `binder-control` | Created, major 243, minor 0 |
| Host configuration changed | No |

The capability mask `0000000000200000` corresponds to `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`.

## **What this proves — and what it doesn't**

We have established:

```
SteamOS kernel
    │
    └── BinderFS support
          │
          ▼
    Rootless Podman
          │
          ├── CAP_SYS_ADMIN only
          ├── BinderFS mount succeeds
          └── binder-control appears
```

This is encouraging, but BinderFS mounting is only the first layer. We still need to test whether the container can create Binder devices and whether those devices can actually be used.

Important security distinction: `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` is a broad capability, not a harmless mount-only permission. Its availability inside this container does not establish that it is safe to grant to a permanent Waydroid service.

## **Next test — Binder device creation**

Let's test `BINDER_CTL_ADD` inside this same isolation model. The test will request the three standard Binder device names:

* `binder`  
* `vndbinder`  
* `hwbinder`

It will not expose devices to the host or alter host `/dev`.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binderfs-device-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    set -u

    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs

    echo "===== MOUNT BINDERFS ====="
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs || exit 1

    echo
    echo "===== BEFORE DEVICE CREATION ====="
    ls -la /tmp/binderfs

    echo
    echo "===== CREATE BINDER DEVICES ====="
    python3 - <<'"'"'PY'"'"'
import ctypes
import os

libc = ctypes.CDLL(None, use_errno=True)
path = b"/tmp/binderfs/binder-control"
fd = os.open(path, os.O_RDWR)

class BinderDevice(ctypes.Structure):
    _fields_ = [("name", ctypes.c_char * 256)]

for name in (b"binder", b"vndbinder", b"hwbinder"):
    device = BinderDevice()
    device.name = name
    result = libc.ioctl(fd, 0x40046201, ctypes.byref(device))
    error = ctypes.get_errno()
    print(f"{name.decode()}: ioctl result={result}, errno={error}")

os.close(fd)
PY

    echo
    echo "===== AFTER DEVICE CREATION ====="
    ls -la /tmp/binderfs

    echo
    echo "===== DEVICE DETAILS ====="
    stat -c "%n major=%t minor=%T mode=%a" /tmp/binderfs/* 2>/dev/null || true

    umount /tmp/binderfs
  '
```

One note: Debian Slim may not include Python. If it reports `python3: not found`, that's simply a missing test utility, not a Binder failure; we'll adapt without installing anything into the image.

## **Test 3 — Create Binder devices**

Temporary rootless container

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binderfs-device-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    set -u

    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs

    echo "===== MOUNT BINDERFS ====="
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs || exit 1

    echo
    echo "===== CREATE BINDER DEVICES ====="

    perl -e '\''
      use strict;
      use warnings;
      use Fcntl qw(O_RDWR);

      my $fd;
      sysopen($fd, "/tmp/binderfs/binder-control", O_RDWR)
        or die "Cannot open binder-control: $!\n";

      my $request = 0x40046201;

      for my $name (qw(binder vndbinder hwbinder)) {
        my $buffer = pack("Z256", $name);
        my $result = syscall(16, fileno($fd), $request, $buffer);

        if ($result == 0) {
          print "$name: CREATED\n";
        } else {
          print "$name: FAILED (result=$result, errno=$!)\n";
        }
      }

      close($fd);
    '\''

    echo
    echo "===== RESULTING DEVICES ====="
    ls -la /tmp/binderfs

    echo
    echo "===== DEVICE NUMBERS AND PERMISSIONS ====="
    stat -c "%n major=%t minor=%T mode=%a" /tmp/binderfs/binder* 2>/dev/null || true

    umount /tmp/binderfs
  '
```

If the ioctl succeeds, we'll have demonstrated Binder device creation inside rootless Podman. That still won't prove Android Binder communication or nested LXC compatibility, but it will clear another important feasibility checkpoint.

Interesting result. BinderFS mounts, but our attempt to create devices returned `EINVAL` (Invalid argument) for all three names.

## **Next step — verify the ioctl definition**

Rather than changing permissions or adding capabilities, let's inspect the exact ioctl definitions on SteamOS.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
echo "===== KERNEL ARCHITECTURE ====="
uname -m

echo
echo "===== BINDER UAPI DEFINITIONS ====="
grep -n -A12 -B5 'BINDER_CTL_ADD\|struct binderfs_device' \
  /usr/include/linux/android/binder.h \
  /usr/include/linux/android/binderfs.h \
  /usr/include/linux/binder.h \
  2>/dev/null || true

echo
echo "===== AVAILABLE BINDER HEADERS ====="
find /usr/include -iname '*binder*' -type f 2>/dev/null
```

This is strictly read-only.

We need to establish the correct ioctl number and structure layout before repeating the device-creation test. No additional privileges, host changes or installation are justified yet.

(deck@Laco \~)\$

Perfect. This confirms the Steam Deck host is x86\_64, but it has no Binder UAPI headers installed under `/usr/include`.

That's not unusual on SteamOS: the running kernel can support BinderFS without exposing development headers in the host's standard include directory.

Our previous `EINVAL` result therefore remains inconclusive. We need the authoritative ioctl definition before interpreting it.

## **Next — inspect the running kernel's exported configuration**

Let's check whether the kernel build exposes its configuration and whether BinderFS-related definitions are available through the kernel's installed metadata.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
echo "===== KERNEL CONFIG SOURCES ====="
for f in \
  /proc/config.gz \
  /boot/config-$(uname -r) \
  /usr/lib/modules/$(uname -r)/build/.config
do
  if [ -r "$f" ]; then
    echo "FOUND: $f"
    case "$f" in
      *.gz) zcat "$f" ;;
      *) cat "$f" ;;
    esac | grep -E 'CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER|CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDERFS'
  fi
done

echo
echo "===== KERNEL MODULE / SYSFS INFORMATION ====="
ls -la /sys/module/rust_binder 2>/dev/null
ls -la /sys/module/rust_binder/parameters 2>/dev/null

echo
echo "===== BINDER DEVICE CONTROL REFERENCES ====="
grep -R -n 'BINDER_CTL_ADD\|binderfs_device' \
  /usr/src /usr/lib/modules/$(uname -r) \
  2>/dev/null | head -40
```

This is read-only. No package installation or host modification.

Why this matters: before we call the device-creation test a failure, we must distinguish an incorrect ioctl request from a genuine kernel/container restriction. We should not grant more privileges to compensate for an unverified test.

Excellent. This confirms an important detail about the Steam Deck kernel:

```
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC is not set
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_IPC_RUST=y
CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_DEVICES=""
```

Your Steam Deck uses the Rust Binder implementation, not the traditional C implementation. Binder devices are not preconfigured through the kernel parameter.

The earlier host-side BinderFS experiment successfully created `binder`, `vndbinder` and `hwbinder` dynamically. That is encouraging, but our container-side ioctl test is still inconclusive.

## **Updated findings**

| Finding | Status |
| ----- | ----- |
| Rust Binder kernel support | Confirmed |
| BinderFS visible inside Podman | Confirmed |
| Mount with `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` only | Confirmed |
| Binder device creation inside Podman | Unresolved |
| Actual Binder communication | Not tested |

## **One important correction**

The previous Perl test used ioctl request `0x40046201`. We should not assume that this is the correct request for the kernel's Binder UAPI without verifying its definition.

The cleanest next step is to obtain the authoritative Binder UAPI header matching the running kernel source, then calculate the ioctl request and structure layout for x86\_64.

We should not install development packages on SteamOS or modify the host for this. The source can be inspected separately.

My recommendation: pause execution tests here and verify the upstream kernel definition first. We have enough evidence to continue the audit, but not enough to conclude that Binder device creation works inside rootless Podman.

The distinction matters: we're trying to establish whether a secure container architecture is viable, not merely whether we can make Waydroid start by progressively granting it more privileges.

before we contine  
please list all the  examinations, inspections, audit, tests we performed, put them in a schema and chart

lets continue

Absolutely. Before continuing, let's consolidate the investigation into a proper Waydroid Container Feasibility Audit — baseline record.

The scope remains unchanged:

* Waydroid must run inside rootless Podman or Distrobox.  
* No native Waydroid installation on SteamOS.  
* No changes to existing containers or projects.  
* No broad privileges simply to force compatibility.  
* A conclusion that secure container deployment is not viable is an acceptable audit result.

Two successful mount experiments

| Test | Configuration | Result |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| B01 | Host, disposable user \+ mount namespace | BinderFS mount succeeded |
| B02 | Host namespace, `BINDER_CTL_ADD` | `binder`, `vndbinder`, `hwbinder` created |
| B03 | Rootless Podman, no added capabilities | Mount denied; exit code 32 |
| B04 | Rootless Podman, only `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` | Mount succeeded |
| B05 | Inspect mounted BinderFS | `binder-control` appeared as major 243, minor 0, mode 0600 |
| B06 | Attempt device creation with Perl ioctl | All three returned `EINVAL` |
| B07 | Check Debian test utilities | Perl available; Python, Ruby, BusyBox, ioctl and strace absent |
| B08 | Check host Binder headers | No matching Binder headers found under `/usr/include` |
| B09 | Check architecture | `x86_64` |
| B10 | Inspect kernel configuration | Rust Binder enabled; C Binder disabled; `CONFIG_ANDROID_BINDER_DEVICES=""` |

Important: B06 is inconclusive. The ioctl request and structure encoding have not yet been independently verified. We cannot call this a genuine kernel or container failure.

# **3\. The ioctl investigation — resolved**

I checked the upstream Linux BinderFS UAPI definition. We have found the problem with our previous device-creation test.

The actual definition is:

* `BINDER_CTL_ADD` is `_IOWR('b', 1, struct binderfs_device)`.  
* The structure contains a 256-byte name, followed by two 32-bit fields: `major` and `minor`.  
* Its total size is 264 bytes.  
* The request therefore needs to encode a read/write operation and the full 264-byte structure.  
   GitHub+1

Our previous request, `0x40046201`, encoded a different ioctl operation and an incorrect structure size. That explains why the kernel returned `EINVAL`. The previous failure was a test-construction error, not evidence that Podman cannot create Binder devices.

Corrected ioctl request

# **0xC1086201**

x86\_64 · BINDER\_CTL\_ADD · 264-byte structure

Previous request

0x40046201

Correct request

0xC1086201

## **4\. Corrected Test 3 — Binder device creation**

We'll repeat the test with the correct request and structure. Same restrictions: rootless Podman, only `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, no network, no host mounts, no device passthrough.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binderfs-device-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    set -u

    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs

    echo "===== MOUNT BINDERFS ====="
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs || exit 1

    echo
    echo "===== CREATE BINDER DEVICES ====="

    perl -e '\''
      use strict;
      use warnings;
      use Fcntl qw(O_RDWR);

      my $fd;
      sysopen($fd, "/tmp/binderfs/binder-control", O_RDWR)
        or die "Cannot open binder-control: $!\n";

      my $request = 0xC1086201;

      for my $name (qw(binder vndbinder hwbinder)) {
        my $buffer = pack("Z256VV", $name, 0, 0);
        my $result = syscall(16, fileno($fd), $request, $buffer);

        if ($result == 0) {
          my ($major, $minor) = unpack("x256VV", $buffer);
          printf "%s: CREATED — major=%u minor=%u\n",
            $name, $major, $minor;
        } else {
          printf "%s: FAILED — errno=%d (%s)\n",
            $name, 0+$!, "$!";
        }
      }

      close($fd);
    '\''

    echo
    echo "===== RESULTING BINDER DEVICES ====="
    ls -la /tmp/binderfs

    echo
    echo "===== DEVICE DETAILS ====="
    stat -c "%n major=%t minor=%T mode=%a" \
      /tmp/binderfs/binder \
      /tmp/binderfs/vndbinder \
      /tmp/binderfs/hwbinder 2>/dev/null || true

    umount /tmp/binderfs
  '
```

## **5\. Updated audit status**

| Capability | Current status |
| ----- | ----- |
| Host BinderFS | Passed |
| Rootless Podman BinderFS mount | Passed with `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` |
| Binder control device | Passed |
| Binder device creation | Corrected test pending |
| Binder communication | Not tested |
| Nested LXC | Not tested |
| GPU / Wayland integration | Not tested |
| Android networking | Not tested |

The next output will tell us whether Binder device creation itself works inside this container. We can then move on to the harder questions: device access, Binder communication and nested LXC.

\>   
\>         if (\$result \== 0\) {  
\>           my (\$major, \$minor) \= unpack("x256VV", \$buffer);  
\>           printf "%s: CREATED — major=%u minor=%u\\n",  
\>             \$name, \$major, \$minor;  
\>         } else {  
\>           printf "%s: FAILED — errno=%d (%s)\\n",  
\>             $name,0+$\!, "\$\!";  
\>         }  
\>       }  
\>   
\>       close(\$fd);  
\>     '\\''  
\>   
\>     echo  
\>     echo "===== RESULTING BINDER DEVICES \====="  
\>     ls \-la /tmp/binderfs  
\>   
\>     echo  
\>     echo "===== DEVICE DETAILS \====="  
\>     stat \-c "%n major=%t minor=%T mode=%a" \\  
\>       /tmp/binderfs/binder \\  
\>       /tmp/binderfs/vndbinder \\  
\>       /tmp/binderfs/hwbinder 2\>/dev/null \|\| true  
\>   
\>     umount /tmp/binderfs  
\>   '  
\===== MOUNT BINDERFS \=====

\===== CREATE BINDER DEVICES \=====  
binder: CREATED — major=243 minor=1  
vndbinder: CREATED — major=243 minor=2  
hwbinder: CREATED — major=243 minor=3

\===== RESULTING BINDER DEVICES \=====  
total 4  
drwxr-xr-x 3 root root      0 Nov  1 22:02 .  
drwxrwxrwt 3 root root   4096 Nov  1 22:02 ..  
crw------- 1 root root 243, 1 Nov  1 22:02 binder  
crw------- 1 root root 243, 0 Nov  1 22:02 binder-control  
drwxr-xr-x 2 root root      0 Nov  1 22:02 features  
crw------- 1 root root 243, 3 Nov  1 22:02 hwbinder  
crw------- 1 root root 243, 2 Nov  1 22:02 vndbinder

\===== DEVICE DETAILS \=====  
/tmp/binderfs/binder major=f3 minor=1 mode=600  
/tmp/binderfs/vndbinder major=f3 minor=2 mode=600

Binder device creation confirmed

Excellent\! We have now passed another important feasibility checkpoint.

All three standard Binder devices were successfully created inside the rootless Podman container, using only `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`.

## **1\. Test 3 — Verified results**

| Device | Major | Minor | Permissions | Result |
| ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `binder` | 243 | 1 | 0600 | Created |
| `vndbinder` | 243 | 2 | 0600 | Created |
| `hwbinder` | 243 | 3 | 0600 | Created |
| `binder-control` | 243 | 0 | 0600 | Present |

The corrected ioctl returned the expected major and minor numbers. This also confirms that our earlier `EINVAL` was caused by the incorrectly encoded ioctl request, not a demonstrated Podman limitation.

Security qualification: this proves device creation inside the container's BinderFS mount. It does not yet prove that Android processes can open and communicate through those devices, or that a nested LXC container can use them safely.

## **3\. Next test — Binder driver communication**

Before attempting nested LXC, let's verify that the newly created `binder` device can actually be opened and responds to a Binder driver ioctl.

We'll use `BINDER_VERSION`, a harmless driver-information query. It does not start Android, create transactions or change host configuration.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-binder-driver-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SYS_ADMIN \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    set -u

    mkdir -p /tmp/binderfs

    echo "===== MOUNT BINDERFS ====="
    mount -t binder binder /tmp/binderfs || exit 1

    echo
    echo "===== CREATE BINDER DEVICE ====="
    perl -e '\''
      use strict;
      use warnings;
      use Fcntl qw(O_RDWR);

      my $control;
      sysopen($control, "/tmp/binderfs/binder-control", O_RDWR)
        or die "Cannot open binder-control: $!\n";

      my $buffer = pack("Z256VV", "binder", 0, 0);
      syscall(16, fileno($control), 0xC1086201, $buffer) == 0
        or die "BINDER_CTL_ADD failed: $!\n";

      close($control);

      print "Binder device created.\n";

      my $device;
      sysopen($device, "/tmp/binderfs/binder", O_RDWR)
        or die "Cannot open binder device: $!\n";

      print "Binder device opened successfully.\n";

      my $version = pack("V", 0);
      my $result = syscall(16, fileno($device), 0xC0046209, $version);

      if ($result == 0) {
        my ($protocol_version) = unpack("V", $version);
        print "BINDER_VERSION ioctl succeeded.\n";
        print "Driver protocol version: $protocol_version\n";
      } else {
        print "BINDER_VERSION ioctl failed: errno=$! \n";
      }

      close($device);
    '\''

    echo
    echo "===== BINDER DEVICE DETAILS ====="
    ls -l /tmp/binderfs

    umount /tmp/binderfs
  '
```

### **What success would establish**

1. BinderFS can be mounted.  
2. Binder devices can be created.  
3. The container can open a Binder device.  
4. The Binder driver responds to a protocol query.

That would be stronger evidence than merely seeing device nodes. It still would not prove full Binder transactions or nested LXC compatibility.

Send me the output and we'll update the audit register before moving to the next subsystem.

Binder driver communication checkpoint passed

Excellent\! This is a major milestone for the Waydroid container audit.

The container has now gone beyond mounting BinderFS and creating device nodes: it successfully opened the Binder device and received a valid response from the kernel driver.

## **1\. Test 4 — Results**

| Examination | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Rootless Podman | Pass |
| `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` only | Pass |
| BinderFS mount | Pass |
| Binder device creation | Pass |
| Open `/dev/binder` equivalent | Pass |
| `BINDER_VERSION` ioctl | Pass |
| Driver protocol version | 8 |

The test used:

```
BINDER_VERSION ioctl succeeded.
Driver protocol version: 8
```

This is genuine communication with the kernel Binder driver, not simply a filesystem or device-node check.

binder deviceBINDER\_VERSION ioctlProtocol version 8Kernel Binder communicationconfirmed

## **3\. What we have established**

We can now say:

Rootless Podman can mount BinderFS, create Binder devices and communicate with the SteamOS Binder driver using only `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` in this disposable test container.

That is a very encouraging result for the container-only architecture.

However, there are still important distinctions:

* We have not tested Binder transactions between Android processes.  
* We have not tested passing Binder devices into nested LXC.  
* We have not tested Android's complete Binder service environment.  
* We have not established that granting `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` permanently is an acceptable security decision.

In particular, a capability that works in an experiment is not automatically suitable for a long-running service.

## **4\. Updated audit progress**

Binder feasibility

# **4 / 4**

Core BinderFS and driver checks completed

This measures our four completed Binder checks, not full Waydroid compatibility.

## **5\. Next investigation: nested LXC**

Now we should move to the next major uncertainty.

The key question:

Can rootless Podman host the LXC container Waydroid requires without privileged mode or unacceptable host access?

I recommend investigating this in stages:

1. Inspect LXC requirements from the exact upstream source version.  
2. Inspect the SteamOS kernel's namespace and cgroup support.  
3. Run a disposable nested-container feasibility test.  
4. Check whether the required LXC capabilities can be satisfied without broad host privileges.  
5. Only then investigate GPU and Wayland integration.

We should not install Waydroid yet, and we should not use `--privileged`.

Next step: read-only LXC and kernel capability inspection.

## **1\. What the document already establishes**

The document contains the earlier SSH feasibility assessment and the subsequent technical investigation.

In particular, its LXC section already records:

| ID | Existing examination |
| ----- | ----- |
| W01 | Waydroid launches an inner LXC Android container |
| W02 | Binder devices required |
| W03 | Render device required |
| W04 | LXC base configuration and broad capabilities inspected |
| W05 | Cgroup, sysfs, proc and device mount requirements identified |
| W06 | Waydroid network architecture inspected |
| W07 | Host IP forwarding side effect identified |
| W08 | Wayland, D-Bus and audio requirements identified |
| W09 | ADB connection architecture examined |
| W10 | Container isolation limitations identified |

So yes: the LXC requirements and architecture audit has already been performed. We should not repeat that source inspection.

One distinction worth preserving: the document records the LXC architecture and its requirements, but does not establish that nested LXC successfully runs inside our rootless Podman container.

The latest test in this conversation also advances the document's Binder investigation.

## **3\. Let's proceed — nested LXC runtime prerequisites**

Rather than rereading LXC source or repeating Binder tests, the next useful step is to inspect the actual cgroup and namespace environment available inside rootless Podman.

This is a targeted runtime inspection, not another general audit.

STEAM DECK — host SSH  
Read-only

Run:

```
echo "===== HOST CGROUP VERSION ====="
stat -fc "%T" /sys/fs/cgroup

echo
echo "===== HOST CGROUP CONTROLLERS ====="
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.controllers 2>/dev/null || true

echo
echo "===== HOST CGROUP DELEGATION ====="
cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.subtree_control 2>/dev/null || true

echo
echo "===== ROOTLESS PODMAN CONTAINER ====="

podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-lxc-prereq-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== NAMESPACE IDENTITIES ====="
    for ns in user pid mount cgroup ipc uts net; do
      printf "%-8s " "$ns"
      readlink "/proc/self/ns/$ns"
    done

    echo
    echo "===== CONTAINER CGROUP ====="
    cat /proc/self/cgroup

    echo
    echo "===== CGROUP MOUNT ====="
    grep " - cgroup2 " /proc/self/mountinfo || true

    echo
    echo "===== CGROUP FILESYSTEM ====="
    ls -la /sys/fs/cgroup

    echo
    echo "===== AVAILABLE CONTROLLERS ====="
    cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.controllers 2>/dev/null || true

    echo
    echo "===== DELEGATED CONTROLLERS ====="
    cat /sys/fs/cgroup/cgroup.subtree_control 2>/dev/null || true

    echo
    echo "===== USER NAMESPACE LIMIT ====="
    cat /proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces 2>/dev/null || true
  '
```

### **What we're trying to establish**

```
SteamOS host
    │
    └── cgroup v2 / namespaces
           │
           ▼
       Rootless Podman
           │
           ├── Private namespaces
           ├── Available cgroup controllers
           ├── Delegated cgroup permissions
           └── Nested namespace feasibility
                    │
                    ▼
               LXC requirements
```

This will tell us whether the outer container exposes the foundations LXC needs, before we attempt to launch an actual nested container.

No LXC installation, no capability escalation, no host changes.

We'll use the results to choose the next test rather than going back over completed work.

Excellent. This is precisely the missing runtime evidence. And it reveals a significant limitation that we should add to the audit rather than repeating the LXC source inspection.

# **1\. Cgroup and namespace audit — results**

Host cgroup v2 confirmed  
Nested LXC constraints identified

## **Host findings**

| Examination | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| Cgroup version | cgroup v2 |
| Available controllers | `cpuset cpu io memory hugetlb pids rdma misc dmem` |
| Enabled in host subtree | `cpu memory pids dmem` |
| User namespace limit | 2,147,483,647 |

The host has a modern cgroup v2 hierarchy. However, not every available controller is enabled in the host subtree.

## **Rootless Podman findings**

| Examination | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| User namespace | Separate |
| PID namespace | Separate |
| Mount namespace | Separate |
| Cgroup namespace | Separate |
| IPC namespace | Separate |
| UTS namespace | Separate |
| Network namespace | Separate |
| Cgroup version | v2 |
| Cgroup mount | Read-only |
| Visible controllers | `cpu memory pids` |
| Delegated controllers | None reported |
| Container cgroup path | `0::/` |

The important combination is:

* The container sees its own cgroup namespace.  
* Its cgroup filesystem is mounted read-only.  
* No controllers are delegated through `cgroup.subtree_control`.

This is a serious obstacle for nested LXC, particularly if LXC expects to create and manage cgroups for the Android container.

It does not yet prove that LXC cannot run: LXC can operate in different configurations, including configurations with reduced cgroup functionality. But we have evidence that a normal nested cgroup hierarchy is not available in this baseline.

# **4\. What should we test next?**

I suggest not immediately granting additional privileges or changing the cgroup mount.

Instead, perform a read-only check of the LXC runtime requirements against this exact cgroup situation, then test whether the container can create nested user and mount namespaces.

There is an important distinction:

```
Nested namespaces
       ≠
Nested cgroup management
       ≠
Nested LXC fully functional
```

We have already established that the first layer exists in Podman. We now need to establish whether the kernel permits another namespace level from inside it, without escalating privileges.

## **Next test — nested namespace creation**

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-nested-ns-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== NAMESPACE TOOL ====="
    command -v unshare || true

    echo
    echo "===== NESTED USER + MOUNT NAMESPACE ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user --mount sh -c "
      echo Nested user namespace:
      readlink /proc/self/ns/user

      echo Nested mount namespace:
      readlink /proc/self/ns/mnt

      echo Identity:
      id
    "
    echo "User/mount namespace exit code: $?"

    echo
    echo "===== NESTED PID NAMESPACE ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user --pid --fork sh -c "
      echo Nested PID namespace:
      readlink /proc/self/ns/pid

      echo PID:
      echo \$\$
    "
    echo "PID namespace exit code: $?"
  '
```

This test creates no persistent resources and makes no host changes.

Why this is the right next step: LXC relies on namespaces as well as cgroups. We already know the cgroup hierarchy is constrained. We should determine whether nested namespace creation works before considering any alternative cgroup strategy.

And yes, I'll keep the audit register as our single progress reference from here, so we don't loop back over completed examinations.

(deck@Laco \~)\$ podman run \--rm \\  
\>   \--name waydroid-nested-ns-audit \\  
\>   \--network=none \\  
\>   \--cap-drop=ALL \\  
\>   \--security-opt=no-new-privileges \\  
\>   docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \\  
\>   sh \-c '  
\>     echo "===== NAMESPACE TOOL \====="  
\>     command \-v unshare \|\| true  
\>   
\>     echo  
\>     echo "===== NESTED USER \+ MOUNT NAMESPACE \====="  
\>     unshare \--user \--map-root-user \--mount sh \-c "  
\>       echo Nested user namespace:  
\>       readlink /proc/self/ns/user  
\>   
\>       echo Nested mount namespace:  
\>       readlink /proc/self/ns/mnt  
\>   
\>       echo Identity:  
\>       id  
\>     "  
\>     echo "User/mount namespace exit code: \$?"  
\>   
\>     echo  
\>     echo "===== NESTED PID NAMESPACE \====="  
\>     unshare \--user \--map-root-user \--pid \--fork sh \-c "  
\>       echo Nested PID namespace:  
\>       readlink /proc/self/ns/pid  
\>   
\>       echo PID:  
\>       echo \\\$\\\$  
\>     "  
\>     echo "PID namespace exit code: \$?"  
\>   '  
\===== NAMESPACE TOOL \=====  
/usr/bin/unshare

\===== NESTED USER \+ MOUNT NAMESPACE \=====  
unshare: write failed /proc/self/uid\_map: Operation not permitted  
User/mount namespace exit code: 1

\===== NESTED PID NAMESPACE \=====  
unshare: write failed /proc/self/uid\_map: Operation not permitted  
PID namespace exit code: 1  
(deck@Laco \~)\$

That's a clear result — and an important one. Both nested namespace tests failed at the same point, before the mount or PID namespace could even be created.

# **1\. Nested namespace audit**

Failed — nested user namespace creation

| Test | Result | Evidence |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `unshare` available | Passed | `/usr/bin/unshare` |
| Nested user namespace | Failed | `uid_map: Operation not permitted` |
| Nested mount namespace | Not reached | User namespace setup failed first |
| Nested PID namespace | Not reached | Same `uid_map` failure |

The critical error:

```
unshare: write failed /proc/self/uid_map: Operation not permitted
```

This means the outer rootless Podman container cannot establish the additional UID mapping required by these nested `unshare` commands.

Important distinction: This does not prove every possible LXC configuration is impossible. It proves that this standard nested-user-namespace route is unavailable with our current restrictions.
