# **4\. My recommendation**

Do not increase privileges yet.

We have already demonstrated that Binder works, but two separate parts of the nested-container architecture are constrained:

1. No writable delegated cgroup hierarchy.  
2. Nested user namespace creation fails.

The next useful step is a read-only inspection of the outer container's user namespace mappings and security restrictions. This may tell us whether the failure is specifically caused by Podman's rootless namespace configuration or by a broader SteamOS/kernel restriction.

That distinction matters before deciding whether a different rootless Podman configuration is worth testing.

No host settings, existing containers, images, or projects need to be changed.

I see you are summarizing after each, good move, lets keep track after each time and update the audit journey, it should help your memory and accuracy 

proceed,

Agreed. From now on, we'll maintain one cumulative Waydroid feasibility audit, updating it after every test. That gives us a traceable engineering record instead of repeatedly investigating the same ground.

We'll proceed with the next question:

Why does nested user-namespace creation fail?

We want to distinguish between:

* restrictions imposed by the outer rootless Podman container;  
* kernel-level restrictions;  
* missing or unavailable UID/GID mappings;  
* security policies such as seccomp or `no_new_privs`.

No privilege escalation or configuration changes in this step.

## **Test 1\. Outer container security and namespace inspection**

Read-only · disposable container

STEAM DECK — host SSH

Run this command:

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-namespace-policy-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== OUTER USER NAMESPACE ====="
    cat /proc/self/uid_map
    echo
    cat /proc/self/gid_map

    echo
    echo "===== PROCESS SECURITY STATUS ====="
    grep -E "^(Uid|Gid|CapInh|CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp|Seccomp_filters):" /proc/self/status

    echo
    echo "===== LSM SECURITY PROFILE ====="
    cat /proc/self/attr/current 2>/dev/null || echo "Not available"

    echo
    echo "===== USER NAMESPACE KERNEL LIMITS ====="
    for f in \
      /proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces \
      /proc/sys/kernel/unprivileged_userns_clone \
      /proc/sys/user/max_mnt_namespaces \
      /proc/sys/user/max_pid_namespaces
    do
      if [ -r "$f" ]; then
        printf "%s = " "$f"
        cat "$f"
      else
        echo "$f = unavailable"
      fi
    done

    echo
    echo "===== NAMESPACE IDENTIFIERS ====="
    for n in user pid mnt cgroup net ipc uts; do
      printf "%-8s " "$n"
      readlink "/proc/self/ns/$n"
    done
  '
```

## **What this test establishes**

| Inspection | What it tells us |
| ----- | ----- |
| UID/GID maps | Which identities the outer container can represent |
| Capabilities | Which Linux capabilities are available |
| `NoNewPrivs` | Whether privilege gains are prohibited |
| Seccomp | Whether a syscall filter is active |
| LSM profile | Whether an additional security policy is active |
| Namespace limits | Whether the kernel reports namespace creation limits |
| Namespace identifiers | Which namespaces the outer container currently occupies |

One important detail: `Seccomp: 2` means a seccomp filter is active, but does not by itself prove that seccomp blocked `unshare()`.

| Host environment | Complete | Inspected |
| Rootless Podman | Complete | Rootless, UID/GID mappings established |
| Waydroid/LXC architecture | Complete | Requirements identified |
| BinderFS | Passed | Mount and device creation work |
| Binder driver | Passed | Protocol version 8 |
| Cgroup v2 | Inspected | Read-only, no delegated controllers |
| Nested user namespace | Failed | `uid_map: Operation not permitted` |
| Nested mount namespace | Not reached | User namespace failed first |
| Nested PID namespace | Not reached | User namespace failed first |
| Outer namespace security policy | Pending | This test |
| Nested LXC runtime | Not tested | Actual runtime still unknown |

Excellent. We now have a much clearer picture. The important finding is that the outer container has a valid UID/GID mapping, but its capability bounding set is completely empty and `no_new_privs` is enabled.

This narrows the investigation considerably.

# **1\. Interpretation of the results**

Outer namespace mappings confirmed

### **UID/GID mappings**

```
0       1000          1
1     100000      65536
```

The container has a valid rootless mapping:

* Container root maps to host user `deck` (UID 1000).  
* Additional container IDs map to the subordinate UID range.

So this is not simply a missing outer UID mapping.

### **Security restrictions**

| Setting | Result | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `CapEff` | `0` | No effective capabilities |
| `CapBnd` | `0` | No capabilities available in the bounding set |
| `NoNewPrivs` | `1` | Processes cannot gain privileges through exec |
| `Seccomp` | `2` | Seccomp filtering active |
| `Seccomp_filters` | `1` | One filter attached |
| LSM profile | Unavailable | No conclusion possible |

Important: We cannot yet say whether seccomp, the empty capability set, or another kernel permission check caused the `uid_map` failure. These restrictions overlap.

### **Namespace limits**

All three readable namespace limits report:

```
2147483647
```

There is no indication of a restrictive namespace-count limit.

### **Namespace isolation**

The container occupies separate user, PID, mount, cgroup, network, IPC and UTS namespaces. This confirms the outer isolation structure, not the ability to create another nested layer.

| Nested PID namespace | Not reached | Blocked by user namespace failure |
| Outer security policy | Inspected | Empty capabilities, NNP, seccomp active |
| Actual LXC runtime | Not tested | Still unknown |

# **3\. Next test: isolate the seccomp contribution**

Before considering any configuration change, we should determine whether the default Podman seccomp filter is involved.

We'll compare two disposable containers:

* A: Current security configuration.  
* B: Same configuration, but seccomp filtering disabled for this isolated diagnostic test.

Both retain:

* `--cap-drop=ALL`  
* `--security-opt=no-new-privileges`  
* `--network=none`  
* Rootless execution

No privileged mode, no host modifications, no persistent files.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
for profile in default unconfined; do
  echo
  echo "========== SECCOMP: $profile =========="

  if [ "$profile" = "default" ]; then
    podman run --rm \
      --name waydroid-seccomp-default \
      --network=none \
      --cap-drop=ALL \
      --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
      docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
      sh -c '
        grep -E "^(NoNewPrivs|Seccomp|Seccomp_filters):" /proc/self/status
        unshare --user --map-root-user true
        echo "unshare exit code: $?"
      '
  else
    podman run --rm \
      --name waydroid-seccomp-unconfined \
      --network=none \
      --cap-drop=ALL \
      --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
      --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
      docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
      sh -c '
        grep -E "^(NoNewPrivs|Seccomp|Seccomp_filters):" /proc/self/status
        unshare --user --map-root-user true
        echo "unshare exit code: $?"
      '
  fi
done
```

Diagnostic only

The second test relaxes one container security layer temporarily. It does not grant capabilities or change the host's security configuration. It is not a proposed deployment setting.

How we'll interpret it:

* If both fail identically, seccomp is unlikely to be the decisive restriction.  
* If the second succeeds, seccomp is implicated; we still would not automatically accept disabling it for Waydroid.  
* If the outcomes differ in another way, we'll investigate that evidence before proceeding.

This is the next useful distinction. We won't change the architecture based on assumptions.

Excellent. This is a particularly useful result: disabling seccomp did not restore nested user-namespace creation.

We have now ruled out Podman's default seccomp filter as the sole cause of this failure.

# **1\. Seccomp comparison**

Diagnostic comparison completed

| Setting | Default | Seccomp unconfined |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| `NoNewPrivs` | 1 | 1 |
| `Seccomp` | 2 | 0 |
| `Seccomp_filters` | 1 | 0 |
| Nested `unshare` | Failed | Failed |
| Error | `uid_map: Operation not permitted` | Same |

The important observation:

* Seccomp was successfully disabled in test B.  
* The exact same namespace mapping failure remained.  
* Therefore, the default seccomp filter is not the decisive blocker by itself.

What remains: `no_new_privs`, the empty capability bounding set, rootless user-namespace restrictions, or another kernel permission check.

We should not disable more security controls indiscriminately.

| Capability contribution | Unknown | Bounding set empty |

# **3\. Next test: isolate `no_new_privs`**

We should change only one variable again.

Compare:

* A: `no_new_privs` enabled.  
* B: `no_new_privs` omitted.

Both tests retain:

* Rootless Podman.  
* `--network=none`.  
* `--cap-drop=ALL`.  
* Seccomp explicitly unconfined.

No added capabilities, no host changes, no persistent data.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
for profile in protected relaxed; do
  echo
  echo "========== NO_NEW_PRIVS: $profile =========="

  if [ "$profile" = "protected" ]; then
    podman run --rm \
      --name waydroid-nnp-protected \
      --network=none \
      --cap-drop=ALL \
      --security-opt=no-new-privileges \
      --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
      docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
      sh -c '
        grep -E "^(NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status
        unshare --user --map-root-user true
        echo "unshare exit code: $?"
      '
  else
    podman run --rm \
      --name waydroid-nnp-relaxed \
      --network=none \
      --cap-drop=ALL \
      --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
      docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
      sh -c '
        grep -E "^(NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status
        unshare --user --map-root-user true
        echo "unshare exit code: $?"
      '
  fi
done
```

Diagnostic only

The relaxed test omits `no_new_privs` temporarily. It does not add capabilities or make the container privileged.

If both fail, we'll have narrowed the cause further without having to weaken the actual deployment.

Send the output and we'll update revision 05\.

## **3\. Next investigation: inspect Podman's rootless user namespace configuration**

Rather than relax more security controls, let's inspect the host-side configuration that establishes the outer namespace.

We need to know whether Podman is using its normal rootless namespace setup and whether its runtime exposes any nested-user-namespace restriction.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
echo "===== PODMAN ROOTLESS CONFIGURATION ====="
podman info --format '{{.Host.Security.Rootless}}'
podman info --format '{{.Host.Security.SeccompEnabled}}'
podman info --format '{{.Host.CgroupManager}}'
podman info --format '{{.Host.CgroupVersion}}'
podman info --format '{{.Host.OCIRuntime.Name}}'

echo
echo "===== ROOTLESS UID/GID SUBORDINATE RANGES ====="
grep '^deck:' /etc/subuid /etc/subgid

echo
echo "===== HOST USER NAMESPACE LIMITS ====="
for f in \
  /proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces \
  /proc/sys/user/max_mnt_namespaces \
  /proc/sys/user/max_pid_namespaces
do
  printf '%s = ' "$f"
  cat "$f"
done

echo
echo "===== ROOTLESS STORAGE/RUNTIME CONFIGURATION ====="
podman info --format '{{.Store.GraphRoot}}'
podman info --format '{{.Store.RunRoot}}'
```

This is entirely read-only.

Why this step? We have evidence that the outer container's identity mapping works, but not that it can create another mapping layer. Inspecting Podman's host-side runtime configuration is the next logical step before deciding whether a different rootless configuration is even worth evaluating.

No installation, privilege escalation, or changes to existing projects.

## **3\. Next test: host versus container**

We now need one decisive comparison:

Can the Steam Deck host create a nested user namespace directly, outside Podman?

We have previously demonstrated host-side namespace operations, but this exact test gives us a clean baseline against the failing container test.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
echo "===== HOST USER NAMESPACE BASELINE ====="

echo "Current namespace:"
readlink /proc/self/ns/user

echo
echo "Creating temporary nested user namespace:"

unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c '
  echo "Nested namespace:"
  readlink /proc/self/ns/user

  echo
  echo "Identity inside nested namespace:"
  id

  echo
  echo "UID mapping:"
  cat /proc/self/uid_map

  echo
  echo "GID mapping:"
  cat /proc/self/gid_map
'

echo
echo "Host unshare exit code: $?"
```

This creates no persistent configuration or files.

Possible outcomes:

* Host succeeds: the kernel supports nested user namespaces; the restriction is associated with the outer Podman execution boundary.  
* Host fails: we need to investigate a host-level restriction before blaming Podman.

That comparison will give us a much stronger basis for the next decision.

This proves the SteamOS kernel supports nested user namespaces in the host's current security context.

Our problem is now isolated to the additional namespace boundary created by rootless Podman, or the way its runtime configures that boundary.

It is not a general SteamOS prohibition.

The key point: Podman itself is correctly configured for rootless operation, but its successful outer UID mapping does not automatically grant permission to create another nested mapping inside a container.

## **1\. Runtime findings**

Runtime configuration inspected

| Component | Installed configuration |
| ----- | ----- |
| SteamOS | 3.9.5 |

| SELinux enabled | false |

The host reports these capabilities for Podman's own rootless execution:

```
CAP_CHOWN
CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE
CAP_FOWNER
CAP_FSETID
CAP_KILL
CAP_NET_BIND_SERVICE
CAP_SETFCAP
CAP_SETGID
CAP_SETPCAP
CAP_SETUID
CAP_SYS_CHROOT
```

These are not the capabilities available inside our test container: that container explicitly used `--cap-drop=ALL`.

### **A useful distinction**

`podman unshare` successfully displays the expected UID/GID mappings. This confirms Podman's outer rootless user namespace is working.

It does not prove that a child user namespace can be created from within an ordinary container.

## **2\. What remains unresolved**

The host runtime report confirms:

* `crun` is the actual OCI runtime.  
* Podman uses systemd-managed cgroup v2.  
* The host has subordinate UID/GID ranges.  
* The outer namespace mapping is valid.

It does not expose a specific `crun` setting that explains the nested mapping failure. We should avoid changing runtime configuration without stronger evidence.

## **3\. Next test: isolate the capability restriction**

So far, our failed nested namespace tests all used:

```
--cap-drop=ALL
```

Podman's own rootless capability list includes `CAP_SETUID` and `CAP_SETGID`. We haven't tested whether retaining the normal container capabilities changes the outcome.

We'll keep seccomp unconfined and omit `no_new_privs`, as in the previous relaxed test. The only meaningful change is restoring Podman's normal default container capabilities.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-nested-capability-audit \
  --network=none \
  --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CONTAINER CAPABILITIES ====="
    grep -E "^(CapInh|CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status

    echo
    echo "===== OUTER UID/GID MAPPINGS ====="
    cat /proc/self/uid_map
    cat /proc/self/gid_map

    echo
    echo "===== NESTED USER NAMESPACE TEST ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c "
      echo Nested namespace:
      readlink /proc/self/ns/user
      echo Identity:
      id
      echo UID mapping:
      cat /proc/self/uid_map
      echo GID mapping:
      cat /proc/self/gid_map
    "

    echo "Nested namespace exit code: $?"
  '
```

Temporary diagnostic

This retains only Podman's normal rootless container capability defaults. It does not use privileged mode or alter the host.

## **3\. Next test: identify the necessary capability**

The successful capability mask contains several capabilities. Rather than grant them all permanently, we should identify the minimum required.

First, let's decode the successful mask into capability names.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
python3 - <<'PY'
mask = int("00000000800405fb", 16)

names = [
    "CHOWN", "DAC_OVERRIDE", "DAC_READ_SEARCH", "FOWNER",
    "FSETID", "KILL", "SETGID", "SETUID", "SETPCAP",
    "LINUX_IMMUTABLE", "NET_BIND_SERVICE", "NET_BROADCAST",
    "NET_ADMIN", "NET_RAW", "IPC_LOCK", "IPC_OWNER",
    "SYS_MODULE", "SYS_RAWIO", "SYS_CHROOT", "SYS_PTRACE",
    "SYS_PACCT", "SYS_ADMIN", "SYS_BOOT", "SYS_NICE",
    "SYS_RESOURCE", "SYS_TIME", "SYS_TTY_CONFIG", "MKNOD",
    "LEASE", "AUDIT_WRITE", "AUDIT_CONTROL", "SETFCAP",
    "MAC_OVERRIDE", "MAC_ADMIN", "SYSLOG", "WAKE_ALARM",
    "BLOCK_SUSPEND", "AUDIT_READ", "PERFMON", "BPF",
    "CHECKPOINT_RESTORE"
]

print("Capabilities present in successful test:")
for bit, name in enumerate(names):
    if mask & (1 << bit):
        print(f"  CAP_{name}")
PY
```

This is just decoding the kernel capability mask. No system configuration changes.

Once we know the exact set, we can design a controlled test of the relevant capabilities, one at a time.

Important: We will not adopt the full default capability set as the Waydroid deployment policy just because it works. The goal remains the minimum necessary privileges, with a clear separation between what the Android runtime needs and what the outer container is allowed to do.

## **3\. Next test: isolate `CAP_SETUID` and `CAP_SETGID`**

These two capabilities are particularly relevant to UID/GID mapping operations.

We'll retain only these two capabilities, with seccomp disabled and `no_new_privs` omitted. This is a temporary diagnostic container, not a proposed deployment profile.

STEAM DECK — host SSH

```
podman run --rm \
  --name waydroid-minimal-userns-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SETUID \
  --cap-add=SETGID \
  --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CAPABILITIES ====="
    grep -E "^(CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status

    echo
    echo "===== NESTED USER NAMESPACE ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c "
      readlink /proc/self/ns/user
      id
      cat /proc/self/uid_map
      cat /proc/self/gid_map
    "

    echo "Nested namespace exit code: $?"
  '
```

### **Interpretation**

* If it succeeds, these two capabilities are sufficient for this test.  
* If it fails, we know the pair alone is insufficient, and can investigate the remaining candidates.

One caution: this test's `uid_map` and `gid_map` will show whether the child mapping actually succeeds, rather than merely whether `unshare()` starts.

We are getting closer to a minimal-permission profile, but this remains a namespace feasibility test—not an LXC or Waydroid compatibility result.

## **3\. Important methodological correction**

We should not keep adding capabilities to `SETUID` and `SETGID` blindly. The evidence so far only establishes that the tested combinations are insufficient.

A more efficient next step is to compare the successful 11-capability set against the host's rootless Podman configuration and investigate the exact user-namespace mapping rules involved. That may identify why the mapping write is denied without a long sequence of arbitrary capability combinations.

No system changes have been made. All tests so far have used disposable containers.

The next test should be read-only inspection of the exact `uid_map`, `gid_map`, and `setgroups` state around the failed mapping operation. That will help distinguish a capability issue from a mapping-policy restriction before we test anything else.

## **2\. How this changes our testing strategy**

The most valuable lesson is to separate capabilities already demonstrated by your projects from the genuinely new Waydroid obstacles.

### **Evidence we already have**

* Rootless Podman works on the Steam Deck, including outbound networking and DNS.  
* The host kernel supports nested user namespaces.  
* BinderFS can be mounted in a disposable container with `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`; Binder devices can be created and the driver responds with protocol version 8\.  
* A nested user namespace succeeds in Podman with the default rootless capability set, but fails with all capabilities dropped or with only `SETUID`, `SETGID`, and `SETPCAP`.

### **What still needs evidence**

* Why the restricted container's UID mapping fails, and the minimum conditions required to make it work.  
* Nested mount and PID namespaces under a suitably restricted configuration.  
* Whether LXC can run with the actual cgroup restrictions and device access available to rootless Podman.  
* Whether Waydroid can use Binder, the AMD render device, Wayland, audio and networking without unacceptable host privileges or persistent host changes.

## **3\. Updated approach**

I recommend that we stop treating every test as a fresh investigation.

1. Use the existing project documentation as the baseline for host, container, networking, GPU and recovery behavior.  
2. Keep the cumulative audit register as the authoritative record of test results.  
3. Test only unresolved requirements, using disposable containers and the smallest necessary permissions.  
4. Evaluate the combined architecture before creating a permanent `waydroid-box`.

One important security detail from the Pi-hole architecture document: it records that an API password was exposed in earlier terminal output and that rotation was not verified at the time of writing. I did not find a plaintext password in the document content retrieved here, but the recorded rotation status remains an item to verify separately.

Scope note: I retrieved the Waydroid audit and Pi-hole architecture document, but the repository's file-search index did not return results that would let me enumerate and inspect every project document. The Stable Diffusion assessment above therefore uses the established project context, not a fresh line-by-line review of its documentation.

### **Where we resume**

Our next test should address the nested UID/GID mapping failure, not repeat BinderFS, Podman baseline, or host namespace tests. Once we understand that failure, we can make an evidence-based decision about nested mount/PID namespaces and then assess actual LXC feasibility.

No project files, containers, services, or host settings were changed during this review.

Let's continue with the nested UID/GID mapping audit. We'll keep this to one disposable test, with no changes to SteamOS or your existing containers.

So far, the evidence shows that:

* The host can create a nested user namespace.  
* A rootless Podman container can do so with its default capability set and relaxed security settings.  
* `SETUID` \+ `SETGID`, even with `SETPCAP`, did not reproduce that success.

We have not yet isolated which restriction causes the mapping failure. The next test will inspect the mapping and `setgroups` policy visible inside the restricted container, so we can choose the next experiment based on evidence rather than adding capabilities at random.

### **Test 13 — Inspect the outer container's UID/GID mapping policy**

Run on the Steam Deck host (`deck@Laco`). This starts a disposable Debian container using the same restricted capability set as the failed tests. It only reads `/proc` and `/sys`; it does not attempt to change mappings.

```
podman run --rm --pull=never \
  --name waydroid-map-policy-audit \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SETUID \
  --cap-add=SETGID \
  --cap-add=SETPCAP \
  --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== IDENTITY ====="
    id
    echo
    echo "===== CAPABILITIES / SECURITY ====="
    grep -E "^(CapInh|CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status
    echo
    echo "===== OUTER UID MAP ====="
    cat /proc/self/uid_map
    echo
    echo "===== OUTER GID MAP ====="
    cat /proc/self/gid_map
    echo
    echo "===== SETGROUPS POLICY ====="
    cat /proc/self/setgroups 2>&1
    echo
    echo "===== USER NAMESPACE LIMITS ====="
    for f in \
      /proc/sys/user/max_user_namespaces \
      /proc/sys/kernel/unprivileged_userns_clone \
      /proc/sys/kernel/overflowuid \
      /proc/sys/kernel/overflowgid
    do
      if [ -r "$f" ]; then
        printf "%s = " "$f"
        cat "$f"
      else
        echo "$f = unavailable"
      fi
    done
    echo
    echo "===== USER NAMESPACE ID ====="
    readlink /proc/self/ns/user
  '
```

### **Test 13 — Results: mapping policy inspected**

Completed

The output confirms that the failed test was running with the intended restricted capability set and without seccomp or `no-new-privileges` restrictions:

| Check | Result | Meaning |
| ----- | ----- | ----- |
| Effective capabilities | `0x1c0` | `SETUID`, `SETGID`, `SETPCAP` only |
| Seccomp | `0` | Unconfined |
| NoNewPrivs | `0` | Disabled |
| Outer UID map | `0 → 1000`, `1 → 100000` | Rootless mapping is present |
| Outer GID map | Same mapping | Rootless mapping is present |
| `setgroups` | `allow` | Group changes are permitted in this namespace |
| User namespace limit | `2147483647` | No apparent limit obstruction |

Important distinction: `setgroups=allow` describes the current outer namespace. It does not prove that the child namespace's `setgroups` policy or UID/GID map writes will succeed.

This strengthens the evidence that simply having `SETUID`, `SETGID`, and `SETPCAP` is insufficient. It still does not identify the exact kernel permission check responsible.

### **Test 14 — Capability isolation, first half**

The mapping policy looks normal. We'll now narrow down the capability requirement systematically.

The successful test had 11 capabilities; the failed test had only 3\. We'll add four of the eight missing capabilities to the existing three:

* `CHOWN`  
* `DAC_OVERRIDE`  
* `FOWNER`  
* `FSETID`

We'll keep seccomp unconfined and `NoNewPrivs=0`, exactly as in the previous comparison. This is a diagnostic test, not a proposed security configuration.

Run on the Steam Deck host:

```
podman run --rm --pull=never \
  --name waydroid-map-cap-audit-14 \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SETUID \
  --cap-add=SETGID \
  --cap-add=SETPCAP \
  --cap-add=CHOWN \
  --cap-add=DAC_OVERRIDE \
  --cap-add=FOWNER \
  --cap-add=FSETID \
  --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CAPABILITIES ====="
    grep -E "^(CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status
    echo
    echo "===== NESTED USER NAMESPACE TEST ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c "
      echo Namespace created successfully
      echo UID map:
      cat /proc/self/uid_map
      echo GID map:
      cat /proc/self/gid_map
      id
    "
    result=$?
    echo
    echo "Nested namespace exit code: $result"
    exit 0
  '
```

Please paste the complete output. A success will tell us that at least one capability in this added group, or an interaction within it, matters. A failure will direct us to the other four. We won't infer that any individual capability is necessary until it is isolated.

### **Test 14 — Results: the first capability group did not resolve the failure**

Completed

The nested UID mapping still fails, even after adding `CHOWN`, `DAC_OVERRIDE`, `FOWNER`, and `FSETID` to the previous three capabilities.

What this tells us: those four added capabilities, as a group, are not sufficient to restore nested UID mapping. It does not prove that none of them contributes in combination with another capability.

\===== CAPABILITIES \=====

CapPrm: 00000000800001c0

CapEff: 00000000800001c0

CapBnd: 00000000800001c0

NoNewPrivs:     0

Seccomp:        0

\===== NESTED USER NAMESPACE TEST \=====

Namespace created successfully

UID map:

         0          0          1

GID map:

         0          0          1

uid=0(root) gid=0(root) groups=0(root)

Nested namespace exit code: 0

(deck@Laco \~)\$

### **Test 19 — Results: `SETFCAP` is sufficient**

Passed

We've now isolated the capability that makes the difference in this test configuration.

The original three capabilities plus `SETFCAP` alone successfully created the nested user namespace and wrote both UID and GID maps.

| Capability set | Result |
| ----- | ----- |
| `SETUID` \+ `SETGID` | Failed |
| Above \+ `SETPCAP` | Failed |
| Above \+ `SYS_CHROOT` | Failed |
| Above \+ `SETFCAP` | Passed |
| Above \+ `SYS_CHROOT` \+ `SETFCAP` | Passed |

The successful effective mask was `0x800001c0`.

This means `SETFCAP` is sufficient in this specific environment and test, not necessarily universally required. The result also does not mean that `SETFCAP` is needed by Waydroid itself; we have identified a condition for nested UID/GID mapping, not an Android runtime requirement.

### **Test 20 — Is `SETPCAP` also necessary?**

We have a successful four-capability combination:

* `SETUID`  
* `SETGID`  
* `SETPCAP`  
* `SETFCAP`

Now we'll remove `SETPCAP` while retaining the other three. This checks whether we can reduce the capability set further.

Run on the Steam Deck host:

```
podman run --rm --pull=never \
  --name waydroid-map-cap-audit-20 \
  --network=none \
  --cap-drop=ALL \
  --cap-add=SETUID \
  --cap-add=SETGID \
  --cap-add=SETFCAP \
  --security-opt=seccomp=unconfined \
  docker.io/library/debian:12-slim \
  sh -c '
    echo "===== CAPABILITIES ====="
    grep -E "^(CapPrm|CapEff|CapBnd|NoNewPrivs|Seccomp):" /proc/self/status
    echo
    echo "===== NESTED USER NAMESPACE TEST ====="
    unshare --user --map-root-user sh -c "
      echo Namespace created successfully
      echo UID map:
      cat /proc/self/uid_map
      echo GID map:
      cat /proc/self/gid_map
      id
    "
    result=$?
    echo
    echo "Nested namespace exit code: $result"
    exit 0
  '
```

Interpretation:

* Pass: `SETPCAP` is not needed for this nested mapping operation.  
* Fail: `SETPCAP` is part of the smallest successful combination identified so far.

We will then test whether the successful minimal set works with Podman's default seccomp profile, rather than leaving seccomp unconfined. That security check is important before considering any LXC experiment.
